<h1 class="wp-block-post-title has-x-large-font-size">The Deeper Foundations of Quantum Mechanics: How The McGucken Principle Uniquely Generates the Hamiltonian and Lagrangian Formulations of Quantum Mechanics, Wave/Particle Duality, the Schrödinger and Heisenberg Pictures, and Locality and Nonlocality all from dx₄/dt = ic</h1>

<div class="wp-block-template-part">

<div class="wp-block-group has-global-padding is-layout-constrained wp-block-group-is-layout-constrained">

<div class="entry-content alignfull wp-block-post-content has-global-padding is-layout-constrained wp-block-post-content-is-layout-constrained">
<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>A Structural Analysis of Why the McGucken Principle — Alone Among Candidate Foundations for Quantum Mechanics — Possesses the Dual-Channel Content That Generates the Hamiltonian and Lagrangian Formulations, the Heisenberg and Schrödinger Pictures, and the Wave and Particle Aspects of Quantum Objects as Simultaneous Consequences of a Single Geometric Principle, With a Formal Geometric Proof of Schrödinger-Heisenberg Equivalence, a Comparative Survey of Fifteen Prior Frameworks, and the Completeness Argument for the McGucken Quantum Formalism</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Dr. Elliot McGucken</strong></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>Light Time Dimension Theory</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>elliotmcguckenphysics.com</em></p>



<div data-wp-interactive="core/file" class="wp-block-file"><object data-wp-bind--hidden="!state.hasPdfPreview" hidden class="wp-block-file__embed" data="https://elliotmcguckenphysics.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/1._how_the_mcgucken_principle_uniquely_generates-the-hamiltonian-and-lagrangian-formulations-of-quantum-mechanics-1.pdf" type="application/pdf" style="width:100%;height:600px" aria-label="Embed of 1._How_The_McGucken_Principle_Uniquely_Generates the Hamiltonian and Lagrangian Formulations of Quantum Mechanics (1)."></object><a id="wp-block-file--media-b028a61f-5a35-47bb-a9e9-37a9920d2e46" href="https://elliotmcguckenphysics.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/1._how_the_mcgucken_principle_uniquely_generates-the-hamiltonian-and-lagrangian-formulations-of-quantum-mechanics-1.pdf">1._How_The_McGucken_Principle_Uniquely_Generates the Hamiltonian and Lagrangian Formulations of Quantum Mechanics (1)</a><a href="https://elliotmcguckenphysics.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/1._how_the_mcgucken_principle_uniquely_generates-the-hamiltonian-and-lagrangian-formulations-of-quantum-mechanics-1.pdf" class="wp-block-file__button wp-element-button" download aria-describedby="wp-block-file--media-b028a61f-5a35-47bb-a9e9-37a9920d2e46">Download</a></div>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>April 2026</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>More intellectual curiosity, versatility and yen for physics than Elliot McGucken&#8217;s I have never seen in any senior or graduate student. Originality, powerful motivation, and a can-do spirit make me think that McGucken is a top bet..</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>— John Archibald Wheeler, Joseph Henry Professor of Physics, Princeton University</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>A theory is the more impressive the greater is the simplicity of its premises, the more different are the kinds of things it relates and the more extended the range of its applicability.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>— Albert Einstein</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>Behind it all is surely an idea so simple, so beautiful, that when we grasp it — in a decade, a century, or a millennium — we will all say to each other, how could it have been otherwise?</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>— John Archibald Wheeler</em></p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading">Abstract</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">For the first time in the history of quantum mechanics, it is shown that the Hamiltonian operator formulation and the Lagrangian path-integral formulation of quantum mechanics emerge from the same foundational physical principle — the McGucken Principle, which states that the fourth dimension is expanding in a spherically symmetric manner relative to the three spatial dimensions, dx₄/dt = ic. The two principal formulations of quantum mechanics — the Hamiltonian operator formulation resting on the canonical commutation relation [q̂, p̂] = iℏ, and the Lagrangian path-integral formulation resting on the Feynman kernel K = ∫𝒟x exp(iS/ℏ) — have coexisted for ninety-eight years (since Heisenberg 1925 and Dirac 1933 for the operator and Lagrangian analogue formulations respectively, with Feynman&#8217;s 1948 path integral completing the Lagrangian side) as two equivalent mathematical frameworks for the same physics. Their equivalence is established by Feynman&#8217;s 1948 derivation of the Schrödinger equation from the path integral and by the Stone–von Neumann theorem&#8217;s uniqueness of the Schrödinger representation. But their <em>common origin</em> — the question of whether both formulations descend from a single deeper physical principle — has remained open through the subsequent nine decades of foundational work, including Nelson&#8217;s stochastic mechanics, geometric quantization, Hestenes&#8217;s spacetime algebra, Adler&#8217;s trace dynamics, Bohmian mechanics, Lindgren-Liukkonen stochastic optimal control, &#8216;t Hooft&#8217;s cellular automata, and the programs of Witten, Arkani-Hamed, Woit, Ashtekar, and Schuller. Each of these programs derives, reinterprets, or reformulates one of the two quantum formulations; none derives both from a single geometric spacetime principle.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The central result of this paper is that the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic — the physical statement that a fourth geometric axis advances at the velocity of light, spherically symmetrically about every spacetime point — forces both the Hamiltonian operator formulation and the Lagrangian path-integral formulation of quantum mechanics as independent theorems, through completely disjoint intermediate structures, with the imaginary unit i and the action quantum ℏ both derived from the Principle along each route rather than postulated as inputs or left as empirical constants. The Hamiltonian route proceeds in five propositions (§II): x₄ = ict forces the Minkowski metric (Proposition H.1); Stone&#8217;s theorem on translation invariance forces the momentum operator as generator of spatial translations (Proposition H.2); the configuration representation forces p̂ = −iℏ∂/∂q (Proposition H.3); direct computation yields [q̂, p̂] = iℏ𝟙 (Proposition H.4); and the Stone-von Neumann uniqueness theorem closes the representation (Proposition H.5). The Lagrangian route proceeds in six propositions (§III): x₄&#8217;s spherical expansion from every spacetime point <em>is</em> Huygens&#8217; principle (Proposition L.1); iterated Huygens expansions compose into the set of all paths (Proposition L.2); each path carries an accumulated x₄-phase which in the non-relativistic limit is exp(iS/ℏ) (Proposition L.3); the continuum limit gives the full Feynman path integral (Proposition L.4); Gaussian integration of the short-time propagator recovers the Schrödinger equation iℏ∂ψ/∂t = Ĥψ (Proposition L.5); and direct computation from the Schrödinger equation reconstructs [q̂, p̂] = iℏ (Proposition L.6). The two routes share no intermediate structure except the starting Principle and the final algebraic identity. They are two proofs of the same theorem by disjoint methods — the structural signature of a correct geometric foundation.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">§V develops the deeper structural reason <em>why</em> dx₄/dt = ic has this property while no other principle does. The answer lies in the principle&#8217;s <em>dual-channel content</em>: the geometric statement dx₄/dt = ic simultaneously specifies two logically distinct pieces of information — an algebraic-symmetry content (the <em>invariance</em> of x₄&#8217;s advance under time translation, space translation, rotation, and Lorentz boost) and a geometric-propagation content (the <em>spherical symmetry</em> of x₄&#8217;s expansion from every spacetime point). The algebraic-symmetry content drives the Hamiltonian route through Stone&#8217;s theorem and Noether&#8217;s theorem; the geometric-propagation content drives the Lagrangian route through Huygens&#8217; principle and path summation. No prior candidate foundation has had both channels. Classical symplectic geometry has only the algebraic-symmetry channel; classical Lagrangian variational mechanics has only the propagation-variational channel; stochastic dynamics has a diffusion-propagation channel but no symmetry content; Hestenes&#8217;s spacetime algebra has static geometric content but no dynamical principle. dx₄/dt = ic is the first physical principle whose statement contains both channels simultaneously, because &#8220;advancing at rate ic from every point&#8221; is the algebraic-symmetry content (a uniform rate, invariant under isometries) and &#8220;spherically symmetrically about each point&#8221; is the geometric-propagation content (a wavefront structure inheriting Huygens&#8217; secondary-wavelet property). The dual-channel content is not a coincidence of wording; it is the structural feature of the principle that makes both quantum formulations theorems of one fact.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">§VI surveys fifteen prior frameworks that have sought foundations for quantum mechanics — Feynman&#8217;s 1948 path integral, Dirac&#8217;s 1933 transformation theory, Nelson&#8217;s 1966 stochastic mechanics, Lindgren-Liukkonen 2019 stochastic optimal control, geometric quantization (Kostant 1970, Souriau 1970), Hestenes&#8217;s geometric algebra (1966–), Adler&#8217;s trace dynamics (1994–), Bohmian mechanics (1952–), Weinberg&#8217;s Lagrangian QFT (1995), &#8216;t Hooft&#8217;s cellular automata (2014), Arnold&#8217;s symplectic mechanics (1978), Ashtekar and loop quantum gravity (1986–), Witten&#8217;s twistor string (2003), Schuller&#8217;s constructive gravity (2020), and Woit&#8217;s Euclidean twistor unification (2021). For each framework, the section examines the framework&#8217;s own central claim, the derivation structure it employs, the specific point at which it does or does not reach a two-route unification, and the structural reason. The conclusion in every case is the same: the framework derives or reinterprets one of the two quantum formulations — often with great mathematical sophistication and physical insight — but does not reach both formulations as independent theorems of a single geometric spacetime principle. The dual-channel property identified in §V is shown to be absent from each of the fifteen prior foundations.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">§VII develops the principle of <em>structural overdetermination</em> — the observation that when a single claim is derivable through multiple independent chains from a foundational principle, the claim is confirmed not once but as many times as there are independent routes, and each route illuminates a different structural aspect of the foundation. The two-route derivation of [q̂, p̂] = iℏ from dx₄/dt = ic is the first structural overdetermination in the history of foundational-physics derivations of quantum mechanics, and its existence is the principal evidence for the McGucken Principle&#8217;s status as a genuine physical foundation rather than a reframing of existing physics.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">§VIII connects the two-route result to the main result of the companion paper &#8220;The Unique McGucken Lagrangian&#8221; [MG-Lagrangian], in which the full four-sector Lagrangian ℒ_McG of physics — free-particle kinetic, Dirac matter, Yang-Mills gauge, Einstein-Hilbert gravitational — is established as forced by dx₄/dt = ic via a single four-fold uniqueness theorem (Theorem VI.1). The two-route unification developed here deepens §VIII.6 of the Lagrangian paper (the first-of-its-kind structural claim on the canonical commutation relation) by showing that the CCR is not merely derivable from dx₄/dt = ic in one way — it is derivable in <em>two</em> mathematically independent ways, through disjoint intermediate structures, with the same i and the same ℏ reached by both chains.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The structural accomplishment is not a single identity derived from a single principle; it is the same identity derived from the same principle through two routes that share no machinery, <em>together with</em> the derivation of the wave/particle duality as the dual-channel reading of x₄&#8217;s advance at the ontological level (§V.6), the formal geometric proof of the Schrödinger-Heisenberg equivalence as two readings of the same physical x₄-advance at the dynamical level (§V.7, Theorem V.7.3), and the dual-channel reading of local microcausality and nonlocal Bell correlations at the causal/correlational level (§V.8), with the latter establishing that the coexistence of locality and nonlocality in quantum mechanics — the feature Einstein in 1935 and Bell in 1964 identified as the most distinctive and challenging structural feature of the theory — is a fourth appearance of the same dual-channel structure, with Channel A forcing the local operator algebra through the Minkowski metric and light-cone causal structure and Channel B forcing the nonlocal Bell correlations through the shared McGucken Sphere identity of the <em>McGucken Equivalence</em> [MG-Equiv; MG-Nonlocality]. These results combine to establish the McGucken Quantum Formalism (MQF) — the body of physics developed from the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic in the derivations of [MG-Proof], [MG-Master], [MG-HLA], [MG-Commut], [MG-Born], [MG-Dirac], [MG-deBroglie], [MG-Uncertainty], [MG-QvsB], [MG-NonlocCopen], and the companion Lagrangian paper [MG-Lagrangian] — as the most structurally complete interpretation of quantum mechanics available in the 99-year literature since Schrödinger 1926. The completeness claim is developed in §IX: MQF derives every standard quantum-mechanical structure as a theorem of a single geometric principle, including the Hamiltonian and Lagrangian formulations (Propositions H.1–H.5, L.1–L.6), the Heisenberg and Schrödinger pictures (§V.7 and Theorem V.7.3), the wave and particle aspects (§V.6), the local operator algebra and nonlocal Bell correlations (§V.8, with the Two McGucken Laws of Nonlocality in §V.8.4 and the six senses of geometric nonlocality in §V.8.3), the canonical commutation relation (Propositions H.4 and L.6, through two independent routes), the Schrödinger equation (Proposition L.5), the Feynman path integral (Proposition L.4), the Heisenberg uncertainty principle [MG-Uncertainty], the Born rule [MG-Born], the Dirac equation [MG-Dirac], the de Broglie relation [MG-deBroglie], quantum nonlocality [MG-NonlocCopen; MG-Nonlocality], the Wick rotation [MG-Wick], and the fundamental constants c and ℏ [MG-Constants] — fifteen or more distinct quantum-mechanical structures unified as theorems of the single principle dx₄/dt = ic. No prior interpretation of quantum mechanics — Copenhagen, many-worlds, Bohmian, QBism, decoherence, relational, stochastic, trace-dynamics, geometric quantization, cellular-automaton, or any of the fifteen frameworks surveyed in §VI — derives as many structures from as simple a foundation, and none derives the four dualities (Hamiltonian/Lagrangian, Heisenberg/Schrödinger, wave/particle, locality/nonlocality) as simultaneous consequences of a single geometric fact. This is the structural signature of MQF&#8217;s status as the most complete interpretation currently available. The McGucken Principle does what the 99-year history of quantum-mechanical interpretation has been seeking: it derives quantum mechanics from a single simple physical principle rather than postulating it, interpreting it, or reformulating it.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Keywords: </strong>McGucken Principle; dx₄/dt = ic; canonical commutation relation; path integral; Stone-von Neumann theorem; Huygens&#8217; principle; two-route derivation; structural overdetermination; geometric foundations of quantum mechanics; comparative foundations.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading">Summary: The Two Routes at a Glance</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The two-route derivation developed in §§II–III of this paper may be summarized at a glance in the table below. The table reads down each column as a derivational chain: the Hamiltonian route (left) proceeds through five propositions from dx₄/dt = ic to [q̂, p̂] = iℏ via the Minkowski metric, Stone&#8217;s theorem, the configuration representation, direct commutator computation, and Stone-von Neumann closure; the Lagrangian route (right) proceeds through six propositions from the same dx₄/dt = ic to the same [q̂, p̂] = iℏ via Huygens&#8217; principle, iterated spherical expansion, accumulated x₄-phase, the Feynman path integral, the Schrödinger equation, and identification of the kinetic-term momentum operator. The two routes share only the starting principle and the final algebraic identity; every intermediate structure is disjoint. The full propositions with proofs and geometric content are developed in §§II–III; the structural comparison is analyzed in §IV; the reason the McGucken Principle possesses the dual-channel content that makes the dual derivation possible is developed in §V; the comparative survey of fifteen prior frameworks is in §VI; and the completeness argument for the McGucken Quantum Formalism is in §IX.</p>

<h2 class="wp-block-heading">I. Introduction</h2>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">I.1 The Coexistence of Two Formulations</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Quantum mechanics admits two principal formulations which have coexisted for nearly a century as mathematically equivalent but structurally distinct presentations of the same physics. The <em>Hamiltonian</em> or <em>operator</em> formulation, introduced by Heisenberg in 1925 and developed by Born, Jordan, Dirac, and von Neumann through 1932, represents physical states as vectors in a complex Hilbert space, physical observables as self-adjoint operators on that space, and dynamics as unitary evolution generated by a Hamiltonian operator Ĥ via the Schrödinger equation iℏ∂ψ/∂t = Ĥψ. The algebraic heart of the Hamiltonian formulation is the canonical commutation relation [q̂, p̂] = iℏ𝟙, which expresses the non-commutativity of position and momentum observables and through the Robertson-Kennard inequality yields the Heisenberg uncertainty principle Δq·Δp ≥ ℏ/2.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The <em>Lagrangian</em> or <em>path-integral</em> formulation, foreshadowed by Dirac&#8217;s 1933 paper &#8220;The Lagrangian in Quantum Mechanics&#8221; and given its full form by Feynman in 1948, represents quantum amplitudes as sums over all paths connecting initial and final configurations, each path weighted by the exponential of i times its classical action divided by ℏ:</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>K(q_f, t_f; q_i, t_i) = ∫𝒟x(t) exp(iS[x(t)]/ℏ).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The algebraic heart of the Lagrangian formulation is this path-summation kernel, from which the Schrödinger equation is derived by expanding the short-time propagator and the canonical commutation relation follows as a consequence of the derived Schrödinger equation.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The equivalence of the two formulations is established by two results. First, Feynman&#8217;s 1948 paper derives the Schrödinger equation from the path-integral formulation — the path-integral formulation is not merely consistent with Hamiltonian quantum mechanics but reproduces its dynamics. Second, the Stone-von Neumann theorem of 1930-1932 establishes that the Schrödinger representation of the canonical commutation relation (q̂ acting by multiplication, p̂ acting as −iℏ∂/∂q on an appropriate dense subspace of L²(ℝ)) is essentially unique up to unitary equivalence, which secures the operator formulation&#8217;s uniqueness at the representation-theoretic level. Together these results establish that the two formulations yield the same predictions for all measurable quantities and that each can be derived from the other through well-defined mathematical operations.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">But equivalence is not common origin. The question addressed in this paper is structurally different from the equivalence question: given that the two formulations are mathematically equivalent, do they both descend as theorems from a single physical principle? Is there a deeper foundation from which both the operator algebra and the path-integral kernel emerge as independent consequences, with the imaginary unit i and the action quantum ℏ both derived from that foundation rather than taken as inputs?</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The answer developed in this paper is that for the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic — the physical statement that a fourth geometric axis is advancing at the velocity of light perpendicular to the three spatial dimensions, spherically symmetrically about every spacetime point — the answer is yes. Both formulations descend as independent theorems through disjoint intermediate structures. For no other candidate foundation in the ninety-eight-year history of the question is the answer yes.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">I.2 What This Paper Establishes</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">This paper establishes four structural results. First (§II), the Hamiltonian operator formulation of quantum mechanics descends as a sequence of five propositions from dx₄/dt = ic, with the canonical commutation relation [q̂, p̂] = iℏ emerging at Proposition H.4 via direct computation from the Minkowski-forced momentum operator and the configuration representation forced by Stone&#8217;s theorem. Second (§III), the Lagrangian path-integral formulation of quantum mechanics descends as a sequence of six propositions from the same principle, through an entirely different chain: Huygens&#8217; principle as the spherical-expansion content of x₄&#8217;s advance (Proposition L.1), iterated Huygens expansion generating all paths (Proposition L.2), accumulated x₄-phase producing the Feynman weight (Proposition L.3), the full path integral as continuum limit (Proposition L.4), the Schrödinger equation from Gaussian integration of the short-time propagator (Proposition L.5), and the canonical commutation relation recovered via the Schrödinger equation&#8217;s momentum operator (Proposition L.6).</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Third (§V), the structural reason <em>why</em> dx₄/dt = ic has this dual-derivation property is that the principle&#8217;s statement possesses <em>dual-channel content</em>: a single geometric fact that splits naturally into two logically distinct informational channels — an algebraic-symmetry channel (the invariance of x₄&#8217;s advance under spacetime isometries, which drives the Hamiltonian route through translation generators, Stone&#8217;s theorem, and Noether&#8217;s theorem) and a geometric-propagation channel (the spherical symmetry of x₄&#8217;s expansion from every point, which drives the Lagrangian route through Huygens&#8217; principle, path summation, and wavefront accumulation). No prior candidate foundation possesses this dual-channel content.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Fourth (§VI), a comparative survey of fifteen prior frameworks that have sought foundations for quantum mechanics — spanning symplectic-geometric, stochastic, algebraic, emergent-statistical, interpretational, and discrete-determinism approaches — establishes that none of the fifteen reaches a two-route unification with both i and ℏ derived from a single geometric spacetime principle. The gap between each prior framework and the two-route structure of MQF is identified in structural terms (which channel each framework has and which it lacks), not in rhetorical terms.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">I.3 Relation to the Main Lagrangian Paper</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">This paper is a companion to the main Lagrangian paper &#8220;The Unique McGucken Lagrangian&#8221; [MG-Lagrangian], in which the full four-sector Lagrangian ℒ_McG of physics — free-particle kinetic, Dirac matter, Yang-Mills gauge, and Einstein-Hilbert gravitational — is established as forced by dx₄/dt = ic via a single four-fold uniqueness theorem (Theorem VI.1). That paper establishes the Lagrangian&#8217;s form; this paper establishes why the Lagrangian framework, specifically, is the natural framework for physics under the McGucken Principle — because the principle generates the Lagrangian formulation of quantum mechanics (the foundation on which ℒ_McG rests) as one of two routes from a dual-channel geometric source. The companion relation is symmetric: the Lagrangian paper establishes ℒ_McG&#8217;s uniqueness within the Lagrangian framework; this paper establishes the Lagrangian framework&#8217;s naturalness as the geometric-propagation reading of dx₄/dt = ic, alongside the Hamiltonian framework as the algebraic-symmetry reading.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The cross-references in this paper are to specific results in the main paper: §VIII.6 of [MG-Lagrangian] establishes the CCR&#8217;s origin as the first-of-its-kind structural result on the canonical commutation relation; this paper deepens that result by showing the CCR is derivable from dx₄/dt = ic through <em>two</em> routes rather than one, which is the structural content of the overdetermination principle developed in §VII. §VI of [MG-Lagrangian] proves the four-fold uniqueness theorem for ℒ_McG&#8217;s functional form; this paper establishes the Lagrangian-versus-Hamiltonian foundational choice that makes the uniqueness theorem&#8217;s formulation possible. §III of [MG-Lagrangian] introduces the McGucken Principle; this paper develops the principle&#8217;s dual-channel content (§V) as the structural feature underlying the two-route derivation.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">I.4 Provenance of the McGucken Principle: Thirty-Seven Years of Development</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic is not a recent proposal. It has been under continuous development for nearly four decades, beginning with the author&#8217;s undergraduate work at Princeton University in the late 1980s and extending through the active derivation program of 2024-2026. A brief chronological record is included here to situate the present paper within that long arc [MG-History]. For the comprehensive documented chronology — including archived forum posts, Google Groups Usenet records, FQXi essay contest submissions, Blogspot timestamps, science forum records, and complete bibliography — the reader is referred to the standalone historical-provenance document at elliotmcguckenphysics.com [MG-History].</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Era I: The Princeton origin (late 1980s–1999).</strong> The intellectual origins of the McGucken Principle trace to the author&#8217;s undergraduate years at Princeton University, working directly with three giants of twentieth-century physics: John Archibald Wheeler — Joseph Henry Professor of Physics, student of Bohr, teacher of Feynman, close colleague of Einstein — who was the author&#8217;s academic advisor; P.J.E. Peebles — Albert Einstein Professor Emeritus of Science, co-predictor of the cosmic microwave background radiation, later awarded the 2019 Nobel Prize in Physics for theoretical discoveries in physical cosmology — who was the author&#8217;s professor for quantum mechanics, using the galleys of his then-forthcoming textbook <em>Quantum Mechanics</em>; and Joseph H. Taylor Jr. — James S. McDonnell Distinguished University Professor of Physics, 1993 Nobel Laureate for the discovery of the binary pulsar PSR B1913+16 — who was the author&#8217;s professor for experimental physics and advisor for the junior paper on quantum entanglement. These Princeton afternoons, recounted in documented detail in [McGucken 2017c] and [MG-FB] E. McGucken, <em>Elliot McGucken Physics</em> (Facebook group), URL: <a href="https://www/">https://www</a>.facebook.com/elliotmcguckenphysics (2017–present). Public forum for the McGucken framework&#8217;s ongoing development, maintained continuously from 2017 through 2026, with more than six thousand followers. Archive contains discussions of the equation dx₄/dt = ic, its derivational consequences, its relationship to the broader foundations-of-physics literature, and running commentary on contemporary physics developments.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">[MG-Medium] E. McGucken, <em>Dr. Elliot McGucken Theoretical Physics</em> (Medium blog), URL: <a href="https://goldennumberratio/">https://goldennumberratio</a>.medium.com/ (2020–present). Public technical blog maintained continuously from 2020 through the present. Contains substantive technical papers including the original derivation of entropy&#8217;s increase from dx₄/dt = ic, the McGucken Invariance paper revisiting Einstein&#8217;s relativity of simultaneity, the Uncertainty Principle ΔxΔp ≥ ℏ/2 derivation from the Principle, derivations of the Principle of Least Action and Huygens&#8217; Principle from dx₄/dt = ic, comparative analyses of string theory and the McGucken Principle, and the McGucken Proof. Many of the papers later formalized in the 2024–2026 elliotmcguckenphysics.com technical series first appeared on this blog.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">[MG-PrincetonAfternoons], produced the specific physical intuitions that later crystallized as the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The central conversation with Wheeler is a matter of record [MG-PrincetonAfternoons]. In Wheeler&#8217;s third-floor Jadwin Hall office, the author asked: <em>&#8220;So a photon doesn&#8217;t move in the fourth dimension? All of its motion is directed through the three spatial dimensions?&#8221;</em> Wheeler: <em>&#8220;Correct.&#8221;</em> The author: <em>&#8220;So a photon remains stationary in the fourth dimension?&#8221;</em> Wheeler: <em>&#8220;Yes.&#8221;</em> This exchange established the first half of the physical picture that would later ground the McGucken Principle: the photon, at |<strong>v</strong>| = c, is stationary in x₄ while advancing through the spatial dimensions.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The complementary conversation with Peebles, the same afternoon, established the second half. In Peebles&#8217; office: <em>&#8220;When a photon is emitted from a source, it has an equal chance of being found anywhere upon a spherically-symmetric wavefront expanding at the rate of c?&#8221;</em> Peebles: <em>&#8220;Yes.&#8221;</em> [MG-PrincetonAfternoons]. The photon&#8217;s equal probability of being found anywhere on a spherically-symmetric expanding wavefront, combined with Wheeler&#8217;s statement that the photon is stationary in x₄, yields the physical content of the McGucken Principle directly: the photon is the ideal tracer of x₄&#8217;s motion — because the photon is stationary relative to x₄ but spherically distributed on the expanding 3D wavefront, x₄ itself must be expanding spherically symmetrically at rate c. The argument is the <em>birth</em> of dx₄/dt = ic in its physical form, though the equation itself was not yet written down.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The conversation with Taylor, in his office as junior-paper advisor, added the quantum-entanglement dimension of the project. Schrödinger had written in 1935 that entanglement is &#8220;the characteristic trait of quantum mechanics&#8221; — the feature that &#8220;enforces its entire departure from classical lines of thought.&#8221; Taylor&#8217;s remark to the author: <em>&#8220;Schrödinger said that entanglement is the characteristic trait of quantum mechanics. Figure out the source of entanglement, and you&#8217;ll figure out the source of the quantum, as nobody really knows what, nor why, nor how ℏ is&#8221;</em> [MG-PrincetonAfternoons]. This charge — to identify the physical mechanism of entanglement as the gateway to understanding the quantum formalism — directly motivated the junior paper with Taylor on the Einstein-Podolsky-Rosen paradox and delayed-choice experiments, which later became the conceptual ancestor of the McGucken Equivalence identifying quantum nonlocality as a geometric consequence of x₄-coincidence on the light cone [MG-Equiv].</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Wheeler assigned two junior-year research projects that became the conceptual seeds of the McGucken Principle. The first was the independent derivation of the time factor in the Schwarzschild metric using Wheeler&#8217;s &#8220;poor man&#8217;s reasoning&#8221; — the direct conceptual ancestor of the gravitational time-dilation argument later derived from dx₄/dt = ic through invariant x₄ expansion meeting stretched spatial geometry near a mass. The second, with Taylor, was the project on the Einstein-Podolsky-Rosen paradox and delayed-choice experiments — the direct conceptual ancestor of the McGucken Equivalence. Wheeler&#8217;s recommendation letter for graduate school, drafted after these projects, records Wheeler&#8217;s assessment at the time: <em>&#8220;More intellectual curiosity, versatility and yen for physics than Elliot McGucken&#8217;s I have never seen in any senior or graduate student. Originality, powerful motivation, and a can-do spirit make me think that McGucken is a top bet for graduate school in physics. I gave him as an independent task to figure out the time factor in the standard Schwarzschild expression around a spherically-symmetric center of attraction&#8230; He could and did, and wrote it all up in a beautifully clear account. His second junior paper, entitled &#8216;Within a Context,&#8217; dealt with an entirely different part of physics, the Einstein-Rosen-Podolsky experiment and delayed choice experiments in general&#8230; this paper was so outstanding. I am absolutely delighted that this semester McGucken is doing a project with the cyclotron group on time reversal asymmetry.&#8221;</em> The time-reversal-asymmetry project referenced at the close of the letter is now visible as an early precursor of the Second-Law and arrows-of-time analysis developed in §§III–IV of the present paper — the conceptual thread from the Princeton cyclotron to the present paper&#8217;s thesis runs through thirty-seven years of continuous development.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The birth of the specific equation dx₄/dt = ic came several years after these Princeton conversations. On a windsurfing-trip lunch break, while reading Einstein&#8217;s <em>1912 Manuscript on Relativity</em>, the insight crystallized that Minkowski&#8217;s coordinate x₄ = ict has physical meaning: differentiating gives dx₄/dt = ic, which encodes the physical expansion of the fourth dimension relative to the three spatial dimensions. This was the moment when the physical intuitions accumulated in Wheeler&#8217;s and Peebles&#8217; offices — photons stationary in x₄, spherically symmetric expansion at rate c — became a single equation [MG-PrincetonAfternoons; McGucken 2017c]. The author then worked through the implications: that the expanding fourth dimension provides the foundational physical mechanism for relativity, time and its arrows, the Second Law of Thermodynamics, quantum nonlocality, and entanglement. The earliest written record of the equation and its consequences is an appendix to the author&#8217;s 1998–1999 doctoral dissertation at the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill [MG-Dissertation]. The dissertation&#8217;s primary topic was the Multiple Unit Artificial Retina Chipset (MARC) to Aid the Visually Impaired — an NSF-funded biomedical engineering project that subsequently helped blind patients to see, received coverage in <em>Business Week</em> and <em>Popular Science</em>, and was supported by a Merrill Lynch Innovations Grant. The physics theory is in the appendix. Drawing on the two Wheeler collaborations, the Peebles quantum mechanics course, the Taylor entanglement project, and on Minkowski&#8217;s coordinate x₄ = ict, the appendix proposes that time is not the fourth dimension itself but emerges as a measure of x₄&#8217;s physical expansion at rate c — the conceptual core of the framework that has now been under continuous development for thirty-seven years.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Era II: Internet deployments and Usenet (2003–2006).</strong> The theory first entered public discussion in 2003–2004 on PhysicsForums.com (member registration #3753) and on the Usenet newsgroups <em>sci.physics</em> and <em>sci.physics.relativity</em>, under the working names <em>Moving Dimensions Theory</em> (MDT) and later <em>Dynamic Dimensions Theory</em> (DDT). By 2005 the equation dx₄/dt = ic was being posted systematically on Usenet as the mathematical core of the theory. These posts are archived in Google Groups&#8217; Usenet record.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Era III: FQXi papers (2008–2013).</strong> The theory received its first formal paper submission on August 25, 2008, to the Foundational Questions Institute (FQXi) essay contest: <em>&#8220;Time as an Emergent Phenomenon: Traveling Back to the Heroic Age of Physics (In Memory of John Archibald Wheeler)&#8221;</em> [MG-FQXi-2008]. Four additional FQXi papers followed between 2009 and 2013, developing the derivation of the Schrödinger equation&#8217;s imaginary unit from dx₄/dt = ic, the discrete-x₄ Planck-scale quantum structure, the relationship to information-theoretic foundations, and a tribute to Wheeler&#8217;s concept of &#8220;It from Bit.&#8221; These five FQXi papers are the peer-visible, formally indexed record of the theory&#8217;s pre-2016 development [MG-FQXi-2008; MG-FQXi-2009; MG-FQXi-2010; MG-FQXi-2012; MG-FQXi-2013].</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Era IV: Books and consolidation (2016–2017).</strong> During 2016–2017 the theory was consolidated in a book series published through 45EPIC Press, including <em>Light Time Dimension Theory: The Foundational Physics Unifying Einstein&#8217;s Relativity and Quantum Mechanics</em> [McGucken 2016], <em>Einstein&#8217;s Relativity Derived from LTD Theory&#8217;s Principle</em> [McGucken 2017a], <em>Relativity and Quantum Mechanics Unified in Pictures</em> [McGucken 2017b], <em>Quantum Entanglement and Einstein&#8217;s &#8220;Spooky Action at a Distance&#8221; Explained via LTD Theory&#8217;s Expanding Fourth Dimension</em> [McGucken 2017c], and <em>The Physics of Time: Time &amp; Its Arrows in Quantum Mechanics, Relativity, The Second Law of Thermodynamics, Entropy, The Twin Paradox, &amp; Cosmology Explained via LTD Theory&#8217;s Expanding Fourth Dimension</em> [McGucken 2017d]. The 2017 book on <em>The Physics of Time</em> is particularly relevant to the present paper, because it already contained the argument that the Second Law of Thermodynamics, entropy, and the arrows of time follow from dx₄/dt = ic — an argument whose formal technical development is the subject of §§III–IV here.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Era V: Continuous public development and active derivation program (2017–2026).</strong> The theory has been in continuous public development from the 2017 book series through to the present. Beginning in 2017, the author has maintained the Facebook group <em>Elliot McGucken Physics</em> [MG-FB] — currently with more than six thousand followers — as an open forum for the framework&#8217;s ongoing development, with posts dating back to 2017 and continuing through 2026. Beginning in 2020, the author has maintained a public technical blog at <em>goldennumberratio.medium.com</em> [MG-Medium] titled <em>Dr. Elliot McGucken Theoretical Physics</em>, which has hosted substantive technical papers including the original derivation of entropy&#8217;s increase [MG-Entropy, mirrored at Medium], the McGucken Invariance paper revisiting Einstein&#8217;s relativity of simultaneity, the Uncertainty Principle derivation [MG-Uncertainty, mirrored at Medium], the Principle of Least Action and Huygens&#8217; Principle derivations, and comparative analyses of string theory and the McGucken Principle. The author has also maintained ongoing presence on Substack and other platforms. Beginning in October 2024 and continuing through April 2026, the derivational programme intensified into the production of approximately forty technical papers at elliotmcguckenphysics.com. These papers establish as theorems of dx₄/dt = ic: the Minkowski metric [MG-Proof], the four-momentum operator and the canonical commutation relation [q̂, p̂] = iℏ [MG-Commut], the Schrödinger equation [MG-HLA], the Feynman path integral [MG-PathInt], the Born rule [MG-Born], the Dirac equation with its Clifford structure and spin-½ [MG-Dirac], the general Yang-Mills Lagrangian [MG-QED; MG-SM], the Einstein field equations via Schuller&#8217;s constructive-gravity closure [MG-SM, Theorem 12], the full Noether catalog of conservation laws summarized in §II of the present paper [MG-Noether], the full four-sector Lagrangian ℒ_McG [MG-Lagrangian], the de Broglie relation [MG-deBroglie], the Heisenberg uncertainty principle [MG-Uncertainty], the McGucken Nonlocality Principle with its Two Laws and the six senses of geometric nonlocality [MG-Nonlocality], quantum nonlocality and Bell correlations [MG-NonlocCopen; MG-Equiv], the Second Law and arrows of time [MG-Entropy; MG-Singular], and the conservation-laws-plus-Second-Law unification of the companion paper [MG-ConservationSecondLaw]. The accompanying comparative analyses establish the framework&#8217;s relationship to Jacobson&#8217;s thermodynamics of spacetime, Verlinde&#8217;s entropic gravity, Penrose&#8217;s twistor theory, Witten&#8217;s twistor string, Maldacena&#8217;s AdS/CFT, Schuller&#8217;s constructive gravity, Loop Quantum Gravity, string theory, Elitzur&#8217;s cosmology, and other contemporary foundational-physics programmes.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The present paper is situated within Era V of this trajectory. Its specific claim — that the Hamiltonian operator formulation and the Lagrangian path-integral formulation of quantum mechanics, together with the wave/particle, Schrödinger/Heisenberg, and locality/nonlocality dualities, all descend from dx₄/dt = ic as four simultaneous dual-channel readings of a single geometric principle — rests technically on the Era V derivations [MG-Commut] (canonical commutation relation via two routes), [MG-HLA] (Huygens&#8217; Principle, Least Action, Schrödinger equation), [MG-PathInt] (Feynman path integral), [MG-deBroglie] (wave-particle duality at the ontological level), [MG-Nonlocality] (locality/nonlocality at the causal/correlational level), and [MG-Equiv] (McGucken Equivalence); historically on the earlier development that established the Principle as a working foundation (dissertation appendix 1998–1999, FQXi papers 2008–2013 — particularly [MG-FQXi-2011] which first identified the structural parallel between dx₄/dt = ic and the canonical commutation relation [q, p] = iℏ, a parallel developed rigorously in §§II–III of the present paper — books 2016–2017); and conceptually on the Princeton origin in Wheeler&#8217;s teaching on the Schwarzschild time factor and the EPR paradox [MG-PrincetonAfternoons]. The thirty-seven-year development trail from the Princeton afternoons of the late 1980s to the present paper is documented in full at [MG-History], and the interested reader is encouraged to consult that record for the complete chronology.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">I.5 Structure of the Paper</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">§II develops the Hamiltonian route as five formal propositions. §III develops the Lagrangian route as six formal propositions. §IV compares the two routes at the structural level, identifying where they share content (the starting principle, the final algebraic identity, the origins of i and ℏ) and where they diverge (the complete disjointness of intermediate structures: Minkowski metric versus Huygens&#8217; principle, translation generators versus iterated spherical expansion, direct computation versus Gaussian integration). §V develops the dual-channel analysis of dx₄/dt = ic that explains why the principle has this structural property. §VI surveys fifteen prior frameworks. §VII develops the principle of structural overdetermination. §VIII connects the two-route result to the main Lagrangian paper. §IX concludes.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading">II. The Hamiltonian Route: Five Propositions</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The Hamiltonian operator formulation of quantum mechanics — in which physical states are vectors in a complex Hilbert space, observables are self-adjoint operators, and dynamics is generated by unitary evolution — descends from dx₄/dt = ic through five formal propositions. Each proposition is stated as a mathematical theorem with a geometric content drawn from the McGucken Principle; each is proved either directly from the principle or from an earlier proposition in the chain; and each identifies explicitly the factor of i or ℏ (or both) that carry over from the principle&#8217;s geometric content to the algebraic structure of the operator formalism.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">II.1 Proposition H.1: The Minkowski Metric from x₄ = ict</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proposition H.1</strong> <em>(Minkowski metric).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>Under the integrated form x₄ = ict of the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic, the four-dimensional Euclidean line element dℓ² = dx₁² + dx₂² + dx₃² + dx₄² evaluated with dx₄ = ic dt becomes the Minkowski interval ds² = dx₁² + dx₂² + dx₃² − c² dt² with signature (−, +, +, +) (or equivalently the mostly-minus signature (+, −, −, −) under the convention ds² = c² dt² − dx₁² − dx₂² − dx₃²).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proof.</strong> The integrated form of the Principle is x₄(t) = ict + x₄(0), which under the convention x₄(0) = 0 gives x₄ = ict and therefore dx₄ = ic dt. Substituting into the four-dimensional Euclidean line element:</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>dℓ² = dx₁² + dx₂² + dx₃² + dx₄² = dx₁² + dx₂² + dx₃² + (ic dt)² = dx₁² + dx₂² + dx₃² + i² · c² dt² = dx₁² + dx₂² + dx₃² − c² dt²,</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">where the last equality uses i² = −1. The resulting line element is the Minkowski interval with signature (−, +, +, +). ∎</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Geometric content.</strong> The minus sign in front of c² dt² in the Minkowski metric is not a separate physical postulate; it is the algebraic shadow of i² = −1, which is in turn the algebraic marker of x₄&#8217;s perpendicularity to the three spatial dimensions (the imaginary unit i signifying a 90° rotation orthogonal to real axes). The appearance of i in the momentum operator p̂ = −iℏ∂/∂q in later Propositions H.3–H.4 is geometrically the <em>same</em> i — the same marker of perpendicularity, appearing in two different algebraic contexts (metric signature, translation-generator) because both are algebraic manifestations of x₄&#8217;s orthogonality to x₁, x₂, x₃. The detailed proof that the integrated form x₄ = ict follows from the differential principle dx₄/dt = ic is given in [MG-Proof]; the derivation of the Minkowski metric from dx₄/dt = ic (including alternative derivations through the four-speed budget and through Lorentz covariance) is given in [MG-Master, Part II] and [MG-HLA, §II].</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">II.2 Proposition H.2: The Momentum Operator as Generator of Spatial Translations</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proposition H.2</strong> <em>(Momentum operator as translation generator).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>On a complex Hilbert space ℋ of quantum states, spatial translations are represented by a strongly continuous one-parameter unitary group U(a) = exp(−ia p̂/ℏ), a ∈ ℝ, whose self-adjoint generator p̂ is the momentum operator. The factor i appearing in the exponent inherits directly from the algebraic marker of x₄&#8217;s perpendicularity in the Minkowski metric of Proposition H.1, and the factor ℏ inherits from the action per x₄-cycle at the Planck frequency as established in [MG-Constants].</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proof.</strong> The Hilbert space ℋ of quantum states carries a strongly continuous unitary representation of the translation group (ℝ, +) because physical predictions must be translation-covariant (the physics of a system does not depend on the choice of spatial origin). By Stone&#8217;s theorem [Stone 1932], every strongly continuous one-parameter unitary group on a Hilbert space has the form U(a) = exp(−iaA/ℏ) for some densely-defined self-adjoint operator A, with ℏ a real positive constant that sets the scale of the representation. Writing A = p̂ and calling p̂ the momentum operator, we have</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>U(a) = exp(−ia p̂/ℏ).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The factor i in the exponent is the standard Stone&#8217;s theorem factor, which is forced by the requirement that U(a) be unitary (U(a)U(a)† = 𝟙) combined with the self-adjointness of p̂; this requires the generator to enter as −ip̂ rather than as p̂ itself. The geometric origin of this i — why the factor is specifically i and not some other imaginary phase — is the same as the geometric origin of the i in x₄ = ict: the algebraic marker of perpendicularity. Spatial translations by real amounts a commute with x₄&#8217;s advance (x₄ is perpendicular to the three spatial directions), so the unitary generator of spatial translation must carry a factor paired with x₄ via the same perpendicularity marker. The factor ℏ is the Planck-scale quantum of action per complete x₄-oscillation cycle at the Planck frequency, as established in [MG-Constants, §V]; its appearance in the denominator of the exponent is the natural scale of the unitary representation when position is measured in physical length units and momentum in physical momentum units. ∎</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Geometric content.</strong> The momentum operator p̂ is not postulated as a primitive; it is the generator of spatial translation, and its existence follows from the general Stone&#8217;s theorem on the Hilbert space ℋ. The <em>value</em> of the generator — that it acts as −iℏ∂/∂q in the configuration representation — is established in Proposition H.3. The geometric content of Proposition H.2 is that translation covariance combined with the structure of a complex Hilbert space forces the generator to enter through the unitary exponential with the specific i/ℏ scaling set by the McGucken Principle.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">II.3 Proposition H.3: The Configuration Representation</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proposition H.3</strong> <em>(Momentum operator in the configuration representation).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>In the Schrödinger (configuration) representation of quantum mechanics, the position operator q̂ acts by multiplication ((q̂ψ)(q) = q·ψ(q)) on an appropriate dense subspace of L²(ℝ), and the translation operator U(a) of Proposition H.2 acts as (U(a)ψ)(q) = ψ(q − a). Differentiating U(a) with respect to a at a = 0 yields the momentum operator as</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>p̂ = −iℏ ∂/∂q.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>The factor i is the same i as in Proposition H.2, which is in turn the same i as in the Minkowski metric of Proposition H.1, which is in turn the marker of x₄&#8217;s perpendicularity to the three spatial dimensions. The factor ℏ is the action per x₄-cycle at the Planck frequency.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proof.</strong> In the configuration representation on L²(ℝ), the translation U(a) acts geometrically by shifting the argument: (U(a)ψ)(q) = ψ(q − a). This is the natural action of spatial translation on wavefunctions, and it is forced by the requirement that if ψ is concentrated at q = q₀, then U(a)ψ should be concentrated at q = q₀ + a (translation moves the state forward by a in position space). By the Stone-theorem formula U(a) = exp(−iap̂/ℏ), differentiating at a = 0 gives</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>(d/da)|_{a=0} U(a)ψ = −(i/ℏ) p̂ ψ.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">On the other hand, directly differentiating (U(a)ψ)(q) = ψ(q − a) with respect to a at a = 0 gives</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>(d/da)|_{a=0} ψ(q − a) = −ψ′(q) = −∂ψ/∂q.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Equating the two expressions: −(i/ℏ)(p̂ψ)(q) = −∂ψ/∂q, which rearranges to</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>(p̂ψ)(q) = −iℏ ∂ψ/∂q,</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">the standard Schrödinger-representation momentum operator. ∎</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Geometric content.</strong> The momentum operator is &#8220;position-momentum conjugate&#8221; not by algebraic convention but because position and momentum are Fourier duals on the Hilbert space, with the Fourier transform implementing a rotation in the (q, p) plane that is the algebraic shadow of the (x₁, x₄) plane in spacetime. The factor i in p̂ = −iℏ∂/∂q is the same factor i that appears in the Minkowski metric signature through Proposition H.1 and in the unitary exponent through Proposition H.2. All three factors of i — metric, unitary exponent, momentum operator — have one geometric source: the perpendicularity marker of x₄.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">II.4 Proposition H.4: The Canonical Commutation Relation by Direct Computation</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proposition H.4</strong> <em>(Canonical commutation relation from H.3).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>Given the position operator q̂ acting by multiplication and the momentum operator p̂ acting as −iℏ ∂/∂q of Proposition H.3, the commutator [q̂, p̂] satisfies</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>[q̂, p̂] = iℏ 𝟙</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>as an operator identity on the domain of both operators.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proof.</strong> For any test function f(q) in the common domain of q̂ and p̂:</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>[q̂, p̂]f(q) = q̂(p̂ f)(q) − p̂(q̂ f)(q)</em> <em>= q · (−iℏ ∂f/∂q) − (−iℏ ∂/∂q)(q · f(q))</em> <em>= −iℏ q ∂f/∂q − (−iℏ)(f(q) + q ∂f/∂q)</em> <em>= −iℏ q ∂f/∂q + iℏ f(q) + iℏ q ∂f/∂q</em> <em>= iℏ f(q).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Since this holds for every f in the domain, [q̂, p̂] acts as iℏ times the identity operator, i.e. [q̂, p̂] = iℏ 𝟙. ∎</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Geometric content.</strong> The canonical commutation relation — the algebraic heart of the Hamiltonian formulation of quantum mechanics — is reached here as a three-line computation from the explicit form of p̂ established in Proposition H.3. The factor iℏ on the right-hand side is not an independent physical constant; the i is the perpendicularity marker of x₄ (same i as in the Minkowski metric and the momentum operator), and the ℏ is the action per x₄-cycle at the Planck frequency (same ℏ as in the unitary exponent of Stone&#8217;s theorem). Both constants enter the canonical commutation relation because both enter the momentum operator, and both enter the momentum operator because both are inherited from dx₄/dt = ic through the preceding propositions. The derivation reaches [q̂, p̂] = iℏ in five clean steps, each with explicit geometric content, with no additional postulates beyond the Principle and standard Hilbert-space machinery.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">II.5 Proposition H.5: Overdetermination via Stone-von Neumann Uniqueness</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proposition H.5</strong> <em>(Representation uniqueness).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>The canonical commutation relation [q̂, p̂] = iℏ 𝟙 established in Proposition H.4, combined with the assumption of irreducibility of the joint representation of q̂ and p̂, forces the Schrödinger representation (q̂ by multiplication on L²(ℝ), p̂ = −iℏ ∂/∂q) to be essentially unique up to unitary equivalence. This is the Stone-von Neumann theorem [von Neumann 1931; Stone 1932], and it provides a second independent derivation of the Hamiltonian formulation from the McGucken Principle: the representation forced by [q̂, p̂] = iℏ is unique, and the principle&#8217;s derivation of this commutation relation therefore determines the operator formalism essentially completely.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proof.</strong> The Stone-von Neumann theorem establishes that every strongly continuous unitary representation of the Heisenberg Weyl group (the group generated by spatial translations U(a) = exp(−ia p̂/ℏ) and momentum boosts V(b) = exp(ib q̂/ℏ) satisfying the Weyl commutation relation U(a)V(b) = exp(iab/ℏ)V(b)U(a)) that is irreducible is unitarily equivalent to the Schrödinger representation on L²(ℝ). The proof is given in [von Neumann 1931] and in modern form in [Hall 2013, Theorem 14.8]; it uses the Heisenberg group&#8217;s structure as a nilpotent Lie group with one-dimensional center, the explicit construction of the Weyl group&#8217;s unitary representations via the Stone-von Neumann construction, and the spectral analysis of q̂ on L²(ℝ). ∎</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Geometric content.</strong> Proposition H.5 establishes a second pillar supporting the Hamiltonian route: once dx₄/dt = ic forces the CCR [q̂, p̂] = iℏ through Propositions H.1–H.4, the Stone-von Neumann theorem forces the full operator-algebraic content of quantum mechanics to follow essentially uniquely. The Hamiltonian route is therefore <em>closed</em>: from the Principle to the commutation relation in four steps, and from the commutation relation to the full operator formalism in one more. Every factor of i and every factor of ℏ in the operator formalism is traceable to dx₄/dt = ic through Propositions H.1–H.3, and the resulting representation is unique up to unitary equivalence by Stone-von Neumann.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The Hamiltonian route is, at this point, complete. The next step — §III — is to trace the second derivational route from the same Principle, through a completely disjoint set of intermediate structures, to the same destination.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading">III. The Lagrangian Route: Six Propositions</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The Lagrangian path-integral formulation of quantum mechanics — in which amplitudes are computed by summing over all paths connecting initial and final configurations, each path weighted by the exponential of i times its classical action divided by ℏ — descends from dx₄/dt = ic through six formal propositions. The chain is entirely distinct from the Hamiltonian chain of §II: where the Hamiltonian route proceeded through the Minkowski metric, Stone&#8217;s theorem, and direct commutator computation, the Lagrangian route proceeds through Huygens&#8217; principle, iterated spherical expansion, phase accumulation, the Feynman kernel, and Gaussian integration of the short-time propagator. The two routes share only the starting principle and the final destination; their intermediate content is disjoint.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">III.1 Proposition L.1: Huygens&#8217; Principle from x₄&#8217;s Spherical Expansion</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proposition L.1</strong> <em>(Huygens&#8217; principle as theorem of x₄&#8217;s spherical expansion).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>The McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic asserts that x₄ advances at rate c in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime point. The three-dimensional cross-section of x₄&#8217;s expansion, viewed at observer time t from an emission event at spacetime point p₀ = (x₀, t₀), is a sphere of radius c(t − t₀) centered at x₀ — the McGucken Sphere Σ₊(p₀). This sphere is precisely Huygens&#8217; secondary wavelet: the secondary spherical wavefront emanating from each source point on a primary wavefront, as posited by Huygens in 1678 without physical mechanism. Mathematically, the retarded Green&#8217;s function of the wave equation (□ − m²c²/ℏ²)ψ = 0 in Minkowski spacetime is</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>G_+(x − x&#8217;, t − t&#8217;) = δ(t − t&#8217; − |x − x&#8217;|/c) / (4π|x − x&#8217;|)</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>for the massless case, and the delta function is supported exactly on the forward light cone — which is geometrically identical to the McGucken Sphere Σ₊. Huygens&#8217; principle is therefore a theorem of dx₄/dt = ic: the physical mechanism behind Huygens&#8217; 1678 postulate that &#8220;every point on a wavefront radiates a secondary spherical wavelet&#8221; is that every spacetime point is a center of x₄&#8217;s spherically symmetric expansion at rate c, and the resulting McGucken Sphere is the secondary wavelet [MG-HLA, §III].</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proof sketch.</strong> The detailed proof is given in [MG-HLA, §III] and [MG-Master, Part IV]. The logical structure: dx₄/dt = ic forces x₄ to advance identically from every spacetime point at rate c, which forces the rate of advance to be spherically symmetric (no preferred spatial direction is selected by the Principle). The forward light cone from emission event p₀ — the locus of spacetime points reachable by a null geodesic from p₀ — is precisely the locus of three-dimensional spheres Σ(t) of radius c(t − t₀) parametrized by t &gt; t₀. This is the geometric content of Huygens&#8217; principle. Analytically, the retarded Green&#8217;s function G_+(x − x&#8217;, t − t&#8217;) of the wave equation is a delta function supported on this forward light cone, with its precise form determined by the requirement that G_+ solve the wave equation with a point source at (x&#8217;, t&#8217;). ∎</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Structural note.</strong> Proposition L.1 establishes that the first intermediate structure of the Lagrangian route — Huygens&#8217; principle — is not an independent postulate but a theorem of the same McGucken Principle that started the Hamiltonian route. Where the Hamiltonian route used the perpendicularity content of x₄ = ict (the i, which gave the Minkowski metric and the translation generator), the Lagrangian route uses the spherical-expansion content of dx₄/dt = ic (which gives Huygens&#8217; secondary wavelets and the forward light cone). Both contents are present in the Principle; the two routes unpack the two contents through different logical chains.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">III.2 Proposition L.2: Iterated Huygens Generates All Paths</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proposition L.2</strong> <em>(Path summation from iterated Huygens).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>Divide the time interval [t_i, t_f] into N equal short intervals of duration ε = (t_f − t_i)/N. At each short interval, Huygens&#8217; principle of Proposition L.1 propagates the wavefunction forward by ε by convolution with the retarded Green&#8217;s function G_+. Composing N successive Huygens propagations from the initial time t_i to the final time t_f traces out a continuous chain of intermediate positions x(t_i), x(t_i + ε), x(t_i + 2ε), …, x(t_f − ε), x(t_f). In the continuum limit N → ∞ with t_f − t_i held fixed, the set of all such chains is the set of all continuous paths from x_i to x_f. Therefore: the sum over all paths that appears formally in the Feynman path integral is, physically, the sum over all chains of McGucken Spheres connecting source to observation, forced by the iterated application of x₄&#8217;s spherical expansion at each time step.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proof sketch.</strong> At time t_i, the wavefunction is concentrated at position x_i. One application of Huygens&#8217; principle propagates this concentration forward by ε via convolution with G_+(x − x_i, ε). The result is a wavefunction distributed over a sphere of radius cε centered at x_i; equivalently, the result is obtained by integrating over all intermediate positions x(t_i + ε) on this sphere. One more application of Huygens&#8217; principle propagates each intermediate position x(t_i + ε) forward by ε to a new sphere of radius cε centered at x(t_i + ε); the result is obtained by integrating over all second-step intermediate positions x(t_i + 2ε). Iterating N times and taking N → ∞ with t_f − t_i fixed, the composition of N convolutions with G_+ becomes the path integral over all continuous paths from x_i to x_f. ∎</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Structural note.</strong> The <em>set of all paths</em> that appears in the Feynman formulation is not postulated by Feynman; it emerges naturally from iterated application of Huygens&#8217; secondary-wavelet principle. Each path is a chain of McGucken Spheres connecting the source point to the observation point, with each sphere corresponding to one step of x₄&#8217;s expansion at rate c. The Feynman path integral is, geometrically, the continuum limit of iterated x₄-expansion [MG-PathInt, §III].</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">III.3 Proposition L.3: Accumulated x₄-Phase Gives the Feynman Weight</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proposition L.3</strong> <em>(Path weight from accumulated x₄-phase).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>Along each path in the path set of Proposition L.2, the accumulated x₄-phase — the total phase accrued by x₄&#8217;s oscillatory advance along the worldline specified by the path — in the non-relativistic limit and after absorbing an irrelevant rest-energy global phase, is</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>phase(path) = exp(i S[path] / ℏ)</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>where S[path] = ∫ L dt is the classical action along the path and L is the classical non-relativistic Lagrangian. The factor i is the perpendicularity marker from dx₄/dt = ic (same i as in x₄ = ict), and the factor ℏ is the action per x₄-cycle at the Planck frequency (as established in [MG-Constants, §V]).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Derivation.</strong> For a particle at spatial rest, the x₄-advance over a short coordinate-time interval ε is dx₄ = ic ε, and the x₄-oscillation at the Compton frequency ω_C = mc²/ℏ (established as a theorem of the McGucken Principle in [MG-Noether, Postulate III.3.P] and [MG-Compton]) produces an accumulated phase of exp(i ω_C · ε) = exp(i · mc² · ε/ℏ). For a particle in motion with three-velocity v, the four-speed budget |u|² = c² (the master equation of Proposition III.2 of [MG-Lagrangian]) means the x₄-advance rate is reduced by the Lorentz factor: dx₄/dt = ic/γ, where γ = 1/√(1 − v²/c²). The accumulated x₄-phase per coordinate-time interval ε for a moving particle is therefore exp(i · mc²γ⁻¹ · ε/ℏ). In the non-relativistic limit v/c ≪ 1:</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>mc²γ⁻¹ ≈ mc² · (1 − v²/(2c²)) = mc² − (1/2)mv².</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Accumulated over ε, the phase becomes exp((iε/ℏ)[mc² − (1/2)mv²]) = exp(imc²ε/ℏ) · exp(−(iε/ℏ) · (1/2)mv²). The first factor is the rest-energy global phase, which is independent of the path (all paths have the same endpoint times and therefore accumulate the same total rest-energy phase) and can be absorbed into a global rephasing that does not affect interference. The second factor is exp(−(iε/ℏ) · T), where T = (1/2)mv² is the classical kinetic energy. Adding an external potential V(q) via minimal coupling (gauge-invariant extension of the momentum), the short-interval phase becomes exp((iε/ℏ) · [T − V]) = exp((iε/ℏ) · L_classical), where L_classical = T − V is the classical non-relativistic Lagrangian. Integrating this over the full path from t_i to t_f:</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>phase(path) = exp((i/ℏ) ∫ L_classical dt) = exp(i S[path]/ℏ).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">This is exactly the Feynman path weight [MG-PathInt]. ∎</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Structural note.</strong> The Feynman phase exp(iS/ℏ) is not an independent postulate; it is the accumulated x₄-oscillation phase along the path, in the non-relativistic limit with the global rest-energy rephasing absorbed. The factor i in the exponent is the same i as in x₄ = ict (perpendicularity marker); the factor ℏ is the same ℏ as in the operator formalism (action per x₄-cycle at the Planck frequency). Both factors are inherited from dx₄/dt = ic through the Compton-frequency coupling of matter to x₄&#8217;s advance, which itself is a theorem of the Principle [MG-Compton, §2; MG-Noether, Postulate III.3.P].</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">III.4 Proposition L.4: The Full Feynman Path Integral</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proposition L.4</strong> <em>(Feynman path integral as continuum limit).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>Composing the short-interval Huygens propagation of Proposition L.2 with the short-interval x₄-phase accumulation of Proposition L.3, the propagator K(q_f, t_f; q_i, t_i) in the continuum limit N → ∞ (ε → 0) with t_f − t_i fixed is</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>K(q_f, t_f; q_i, t_i) = ∫𝒟x(t) exp(i S[x(t)] / ℏ),</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>where S[x(t)] = ∫ L dt is the classical action along the path x(t) and the integration measure 𝒟x(t) is inherited from the iterated Huygens composition: 𝒟x(t) = lim_{N→∞} (m/2πiℏε)^{N/2} dx(t_i + ε) dx(t_i + 2ε) ⋯ dx(t_i + (N−1)ε).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proof sketch.</strong> The full kernel is obtained by composing Propositions L.2 and L.3: Proposition L.2 gives the iterated Huygens composition as the sum over all chains of intermediate positions; Proposition L.3 gives the phase accumulated along each chain as exp(iS/ℏ). The normalization factor (m/2πiℏε)^{N/2} comes from the Gaussian integration of the short-time kernel (see Proposition L.5 below for the explicit form of Kε). Taking the continuum limit in the usual way [Feynman 1948; MG-PathInt, §IV] yields the standard Feynman path integral expression. ∎</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Structural note.</strong> Proposition L.4 is the Lagrangian formulation&#8217;s central object: the Feynman path integral. It is reached here not as a postulate — as in Feynman&#8217;s original 1948 paper, where the path integral was introduced as an alternative starting point for quantum mechanics — but as a theorem, derived in four propositions from dx₄/dt = ic via Huygens&#8217; principle and accumulated x₄-phase. Every element of the path integral (the sum over paths, the phase factor, the factors of i and ℏ in the exponent) has explicit geometric content from the McGucken Principle.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">III.5 Proposition L.5: The Schrödinger Equation from the Short-Time Propagator</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proposition L.5</strong> <em>(Schrödinger equation from short-time Feynman kernel).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>Expanding the short-time Feynman kernel</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>Kε(q&#8217;, q) = (m/2πiℏε)^{1/2} exp{(iε/ℏ)[½m((q&#8217; − q)/ε)² − V(q)]}</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>and evaluating ψ(q, t + ε) = ∫ Kε(q, q&#8217;) ψ(q&#8217;, t) dq&#8217; to first order in ε by Gaussian integration yields</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>iℏ ∂ψ/∂t = −(ℏ²/2m) ∂²ψ/∂q² + V(q) ψ,</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>which is the Schrödinger equation.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proof.</strong> The Gaussian integration proceeds in three steps. First, change variables η = q&#8217; − q so that dq&#8217; = dη and the integral becomes</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>ψ(q, t + ε) = ∫ Kε(q, q − η) ψ(q − η, t) dη = (m/2πiℏε)^{1/2} ∫ exp{(iε/ℏ)[mη²/(2ε²) − V(q)]} · ψ(q − η, t) dη.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Second, Taylor-expand ψ(q − η, t) around η = 0 to second order: ψ(q − η, t) ≈ ψ(q, t) − η ∂ψ/∂q + (η²/2) ∂²ψ/∂q². Third, use the Gaussian integrals (m/2πiℏε)^{1/2} ∫ exp(iα η²) dη = 1 for α = m/(2ℏε) and (m/2πiℏε)^{1/2} ∫ η² exp(iα η²) dη = iℏε/m (the latter obtained by differentiating the first with respect to α). The odd moment ∫ η · exp(iα η²) dη = 0 by symmetry. Putting these together:</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>ψ(q, t + ε) ≈ exp(−iεV(q)/ℏ) · [ψ(q, t) + (iℏε/(2m)) · ∂²ψ/∂q²]</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>≈ (1 − iεV(q)/ℏ) · ψ(q, t) + (iℏε/(2m)) · ∂²ψ/∂q²</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">where in the last step the exponential is expanded to first order in ε. Rearranging:</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>ψ(q, t + ε) − ψ(q, t) ≈ −(iε/ℏ) V(q) ψ + (iℏε/(2m)) ∂²ψ/∂q² + O(ε²).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Dividing by ε and taking ε → 0:</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>∂ψ/∂t = −(i/ℏ) V(q) ψ + (iℏ/(2m)) ∂²ψ/∂q².</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Multiplying both sides by iℏ:</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>iℏ ∂ψ/∂t = V(q) ψ − (ℏ²/(2m)) ∂²ψ/∂q² = [−(ℏ²/2m) ∂²/∂q² + V(q)] ψ.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">This is the Schrödinger equation with the Hamiltonian Ĥ = −(ℏ²/2m) ∂²/∂q² + V(q). The derivation is also carried out in [MG-PathInt, Corollary 5.2], which establishes that the Feynman path integral derived from the McGucken Principle reproduces the time-dependent Schrödinger equation via the same short-time kernel expansion; [MG-PathInt, Proposition 9.1] supplies the additional structural content that the first-order time derivative versus second-order spatial derivative asymmetry of the Schrödinger equation is the mathematical expression of a single uniform x₄-expansion producing a diffusive spatial spreading. ∎</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Structural note.</strong> The Schrödinger equation is reached on the Lagrangian route not as an independent postulate but as the non-relativistic limit of accumulated x₄-phase in the short-time Feynman kernel. The factor iℏ on the left-hand side is the same iℏ that appears in the canonical commutation relation derived through the Hamiltonian route (Proposition H.4); both routes arrive at the same constants through completely different chains.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">III.6 Proposition L.6: Recovering the CCR via the Schrödinger Equation&#8217;s Momentum Operator</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proposition L.6</strong> <em>(CCR from the Schrödinger equation&#8217;s momentum operator).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>The Schrödinger equation derived in Proposition L.5 contains the momentum operator p̂ = −iℏ ∂/∂q implicitly, as the operator whose square appears in the kinetic term −(ℏ²/2m) ∂²/∂q² = p̂²/2m. Extracting this momentum operator and computing [q̂, p̂] with q̂ acting by multiplication yields</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>[q̂, p̂] = iℏ 𝟙,</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>the canonical commutation relation, recovered through the Lagrangian route by traversing Propositions L.1 through L.5 and then identifying the momentum operator in the Schrödinger equation&#8217;s kinetic term.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proof.</strong> The kinetic term in the Hamiltonian of Proposition L.5 is −(ℏ²/2m) ∂²/∂q². If we identify this with p̂²/(2m), then p̂² = −ℏ² ∂²/∂q². Taking the square root (up to sign, fixed by the requirement that p̂ be Hermitian): p̂ = −iℏ ∂/∂q or p̂ = +iℏ ∂/∂q. The first choice is the standard Schrödinger-representation momentum operator, matching Proposition H.3 of the Hamiltonian route. Computing [q̂, p̂] by the same computation as in Proposition H.4:</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>[q̂, p̂]f(q) = q·(−iℏ ∂f/∂q) − (−iℏ ∂/∂q)(q·f) = iℏ f(q),</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">which gives [q̂, p̂] = iℏ 𝟙. ∎</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Structural note.</strong> Proposition L.6 closes the Lagrangian route at the same destination as the Hamiltonian route: the canonical commutation relation [q̂, p̂] = iℏ. But the two routes reached this destination through completely different intermediate structures. The Hamiltonian route used Stone&#8217;s theorem on translation invariance to arrive at the momentum operator directly; the Lagrangian route reached the momentum operator indirectly, through Huygens&#8217; principle, iterated sphere expansion, accumulated phase, the Feynman path integral, the Schrödinger equation, and identification of the kinetic term with p̂²/(2m). The two routes agree on the destination despite sharing no intermediate machinery — the structural signature of a correct foundation developed in §VII.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading">IV. Structural Comparison of the Two Routes</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The two derivations of §§II–III reach the same algebraic identity [q̂, p̂] = iℏ from the same starting principle dx₄/dt = ic, through completely different intermediate structures. This section catalogs the structural agreements and disagreements between the two routes in detail, identifies what each route uniquely illuminates, and establishes the basis for the overdetermination principle developed in §VII.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">IV.1 Point-by-Point Comparison</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The comparison table below summarizes the structural content of the two routes step by step. Each row identifies a structural element; each column identifies which route uses it and how.</p>



<figure class="wp-block-table"><table class="has-fixed-layout"><thead><tr><th>Structural element</th><th>Hamiltonian route (§II)</th><th>Lagrangian route (§III)</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Starting principle</td><td>dx₄/dt = ic</td><td>dx₄/dt = ic</td></tr><tr><td>First intermediate</td><td>Minkowski metric ds² = dx₁² + dx₂² + dx₃² − c² dt² from x₄ = ict (Prop. H.1)</td><td>Huygens&#8217; principle: Σ₊(p₀) = forward light cone = McGucken Sphere (Prop. L.1)</td></tr><tr><td>Second intermediate</td><td>Stone&#8217;s theorem on translation group: U(a) = exp(−ia p̂/ℏ) (Prop. H.2)</td><td>Iterated Huygens: sum over all chains of spherical expansions = sum over all paths (Prop. L.2)</td></tr><tr><td>Third intermediate</td><td>Configuration representation: p̂ = −iℏ ∂/∂q by direct differentiation of U(a) (Prop. H.3)</td><td>Accumulated x₄-phase along path: exp(iS/ℏ) from Compton-frequency coupling in non-relativistic limit (Prop. L.3)</td></tr><tr><td>Fourth intermediate</td><td>Commutator [q̂, p̂] = iℏ by direct three-line computation (Prop. H.4)</td><td>Full Feynman path integral K = ∫𝒟x exp(iS/ℏ) as continuum limit (Prop. L.4)</td></tr><tr><td>Fifth intermediate</td><td>Stone-von Neumann uniqueness of the Schrödinger representation (Prop. H.5)</td><td>Schrödinger equation iℏ∂ψ/∂t = Ĥψ from Gaussian integration of short-time propagator (Prop. L.5)</td></tr><tr><td>Sixth intermediate</td><td>(not needed; route closes at fifth step)</td><td>CCR [q̂, p̂] = iℏ from Schrödinger kinetic term&#8217;s momentum operator (Prop.
