L.6)</td></tr><tr><td>Final destination</td><td>[q̂, p̂] = iℏ 𝟙</td><td>[q̂, p̂] = iℏ 𝟙</td></tr><tr><td>Where i enters</td><td>Minkowski signature → unitary exponent → momentum operator</td><td>Accumulated x₄-oscillation phase → path-integral weight → Schrödinger equation</td></tr><tr><td>Where ℏ enters</td><td>Action-per-x₄-cycle scale of unitary representation</td><td>Action-per-x₄-cycle in denominator of path-integral exponent</td></tr><tr><td>Mathematical machinery</td><td>Stone&#8217;s theorem, direct differentiation, Stone-von Neumann theorem</td><td>Huygens convolution, iterated composition, Gaussian integration, Taylor expansion</td></tr><tr><td>Geometric content used</td><td>Perpendicularity of x₄ (i² = −1), translation invariance</td><td>Spherical symmetry of x₄&#8217;s expansion, Compton-frequency coupling</td></tr><tr><td>Dual-channel aspect</td><td>Algebraic-symmetry channel of dx₄/dt = ic</td><td>Geometric-propagation channel of dx₄/dt = ic</td></tr></tbody></table></figure>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The first and last rows show the only structural agreements between the two routes: they start from the same principle and reach the same destination. Every row in between shows a structural difference. The routes use disjoint mathematical machinery (Stone&#8217;s theorem versus Huygens convolution; direct commutator computation versus Gaussian integration of Feynman kernels; Stone-von Neumann versus Schrödinger derivation). They pass through disjoint intermediate structures (Minkowski metric versus Huygens&#8217; principle; unitary translation generator versus path-integral weight; direct algebra versus integral representation). They use disjoint content of the starting principle (algebraic-symmetry content in the Hamiltonian route; geometric-propagation content in the Lagrangian route).</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">IV.2 The Disjointness of Intermediate Content</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">A potential objection to the two-route structure would be that the two chains secretly share intermediate content, making them variants of a single derivation rather than genuinely independent. This objection is precluded by the disjointness catalog above: the Hamiltonian route&#8217;s intermediate structures (Minkowski metric, Stone&#8217;s theorem, configuration representation, direct commutator) and the Lagrangian route&#8217;s intermediate structures (Huygens&#8217; principle, iterated sphere summation, accumulated phase, Feynman kernel, Gaussian-integrated Schrödinger equation) share no common element. The only common elements are the start (dx₄/dt = ic) and the end ([q̂, p̂] = iℏ).</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The disjointness of intermediate content has a sharper form. In the Hamiltonian route, the Minkowski metric appears as the algebraic consequence of x₄ = ict&#8217;s perpendicularity, and Stone&#8217;s theorem is applied directly to the Hilbert-space representation of spatial translations on the background of this Minkowski geometry. Huygens&#8217; principle does not appear anywhere in the Hamiltonian route, nor does the sum over paths, nor the Feynman kernel, nor the Schrödinger equation (the Schrödinger equation is <em>implied</em> by the commutation relation and the Stone-von Neumann representation, but it is not <em>derived</em> in the course of the route; the route derives the CCR directly). In the Lagrangian route, Huygens&#8217; principle appears as the geometric-propagation content of x₄&#8217;s spherical expansion, and it is iterated to generate the path set. Stone&#8217;s theorem does not appear anywhere in the Lagrangian route, nor does the configuration representation, nor does the commutator computation of Proposition H.4 (the commutator is <em>implied</em> by the derived Schrödinger equation&#8217;s momentum operator, but it is not <em>computed directly</em> in the course of the route; the route reaches the CCR through the Schrödinger-operator extraction of Proposition L.6).</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">IV.3 What Each Route Uniquely Illuminates</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Although the two routes reach the same destination, each route illuminates structural features of the CCR that the other route leaves implicit.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>The Hamiltonian route</strong> emphasizes the <em>algebraic-symmetry</em> content of the commutation relation. [q̂, p̂] = iℏ is derived through Stone&#8217;s theorem on spatial-translation unitary representations, and the Stone-von Neumann closure establishes that the representation is unique up to unitary equivalence. The commutation relation&#8217;s algebraic structure, its relationship to the Heisenberg Weyl group, its uniqueness up to unitary equivalence, and its direct derivation from symmetry — all of these are foregrounded by the Hamiltonian route. The route reveals the commutation relation&#8217;s character as an algebraic consequence of translation invariance on a Hilbert space.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>The Lagrangian route</strong> emphasizes the <em>geometric-propagation</em> content of the commutation relation. [q̂, p̂] = iℏ is derived through Huygens&#8217; principle on spacetime, iterated spherical expansion of x₄, and the non-relativistic limit of accumulated path phase. The commutation relation&#8217;s origin in the phase structure of matter coupled to x₄&#8217;s oscillation at the Compton frequency, its emergence through the path integral&#8217;s Gaussian integration, and its connection to the geometric propagation of wavefronts in spacetime — all of these are foregrounded by the Lagrangian route. The route reveals the commutation relation&#8217;s character as a geometric consequence of spherical expansion in spacetime.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Both characterizations are correct. The commutation relation is <em>both</em> an algebraic consequence of translation invariance <em>and</em> a geometric consequence of spherical expansion. The two routes are two proofs of the same theorem from two different structural vantage points, and each proof reveals a structural aspect of the theorem that the other proof leaves implicit.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">IV.4 Structural Agreement on the Constants</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The two routes agree on the factor i and the factor ℏ that appear in the commutation relation [q̂, p̂] = iℏ, and they agree on the physical origin of both factors.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>The factor i</strong> on the Hamiltonian route enters at Proposition H.1 as the i in x₄ = ict (the perpendicularity marker of x₄), propagates through the Minkowski metric signature, enters the unitary exponential of Stone&#8217;s theorem as the i in U(a) = exp(−ia p̂/ℏ), enters the momentum operator p̂ = −iℏ ∂/∂q as the same i, and appears in the commutation relation [q̂, p̂] = iℏ by direct computation. On the Lagrangian route, the factor i enters at Proposition L.3 as the i in the accumulated x₄-phase exp(i ω_C ε) from the Compton-frequency coupling (which itself carries the i from x₄ = ict), propagates through the full path integral weight exp(iS/ℏ), appears in the Schrödinger equation iℏ ∂ψ/∂t = Ĥψ, and is recovered in the commutation relation via the Schrödinger momentum operator. Both routes trace the i to the same geometric fact: the perpendicularity of x₄ to the three spatial dimensions, encoded algebraically as the imaginary unit i.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>The factor ℏ</strong> on both routes traces to the same physical origin: the quantum of action per complete x₄-oscillation cycle at the Planck frequency f_P, as established in [MG-Constants, §V]. On the Hamiltonian route, ℏ appears in the unitary exponential as the natural scale of the representation (determined by Stone&#8217;s theorem with a scale parameter, fixed physically by the requirement that U(a) agree with the physical translation at scale a in units where the Planck action is ℏ). On the Lagrangian route, ℏ appears in the path-integral weight as the scale of the Compton-frequency coupling ω_C = mc²/ℏ in the accumulated phase, which is directly the Planck-action scale. Both routes identify ℏ with the same physical quantity: the action per x₄-cycle at the Planck scale.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">IV.5 Summary of the Structural Comparison</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The two routes agree on the starting principle (dx₄/dt = ic), on the final destination ([q̂, p̂] = iℏ), and on the physical origins of the factors i and ℏ in that destination. They disagree on — or rather, are disjoint on — every intermediate structure: mathematical machinery, intermediate theorems, logical steps, geometric content used. The disjointness of intermediate structure combined with the agreement on start and end points is the precise structural signature of a correct physical foundation developed in §VII: when two independent chains from a single principle reach the same algebraic identity through disjoint routes, the identity is not an artifact of derivational choice but a genuine consequence of the principle, and each chain confirms the identity by a disjoint body of evidence.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading">V. Why dx₄/dt = ic Has This Property</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The two-route derivation of §§II–III establishes that both the Hamiltonian and Lagrangian formulations of quantum mechanics descend as theorems from dx₄/dt = ic. This section addresses the deeper question: <em>why does this principle, specifically, possess this structural property?</em> The answer developed here is that dx₄/dt = ic is a principle with <em>dual-channel content</em> — a single geometric statement that simultaneously specifies two logically distinct pieces of information, each of which drives one of the two routes. No prior candidate foundation for quantum mechanics has had this dual-channel content, and the principle&#8217;s possession of it is what makes the two-route derivation possible.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">V.1 The Two Channels Identified</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The geometric statement <em>&#8220;dx₄/dt = ic&#8221;</em> combined with the physical interpretation <em>&#8220;x₄ advances at the velocity of light from every spacetime point, spherically symmetrically about each point&#8221;</em> contains two logically distinct pieces of information. The decomposition of the principle into its two components can be stated as follows.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Channel A (Algebraic-symmetry channel).</strong> The principle specifies that x₄&#8217;s advance has a <em>uniform rate</em> ic that is <em>invariant</em> under spacetime isometries: the advance rate is the same at every spacetime point (translation invariance), independent of direction in the three spatial dimensions (rotation invariance), and form-invariant under Lorentz boost (Lorentz invariance). These invariances generate the Poincaré-group symmetries of the Minkowski spacetime derived in Proposition III.1 of [MG-Lagrangian] and the ten Poincaré conservation laws derived in [MG-Noether]. The channel&#8217;s content — uniformity plus invariance — is precisely the content needed to apply Stone&#8217;s theorem to the unitary representations of the spacetime symmetry group, which drives the Hamiltonian route of §II. The factor i in the unitary generators, the factor ℏ as the scale of the representation, the canonical commutation relation as a consequence of translation invariance — all of these are consequences of the algebraic-symmetry channel.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Channel B (Geometric-propagation channel).</strong> The principle specifies that x₄&#8217;s advance is <em>spherically symmetric</em> about every spacetime point — the advance at rate c radiates equally into all three-dimensional directions from each point of emission. This spherical symmetry generates the McGucken Sphere geometry of [MG-Master, Part IV], which is precisely the forward light cone of Minkowski spacetime and which is precisely Huygens&#8217; secondary-wavelet structure. The channel&#8217;s content — spherical emission from every point with radial rate c — is precisely the content needed to generate Huygens&#8217; principle as a theorem (Proposition L.1) and to iterate the Huygens expansion into a sum over paths (Proposition L.2), which drives the Lagrangian route of §III. The Feynman path integral as a sum over chains of McGucken Spheres, the accumulated x₄-phase along each path, the Schrödinger equation from Gaussian integration — all of these are consequences of the geometric-propagation channel.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Both channels are present in the principle&#8217;s single geometric statement. &#8220;x₄ advances at ic from every point&#8221; is the algebraic-symmetry content (uniform rate, invariance under spacetime translations). &#8220;Spherically symmetrically about each point&#8221; is the geometric-propagation content (isotropic wavefront expansion, Huygens&#8217; secondary wavelets). The two channels are not alternative readings of the principle; they are <em>simultaneously valid readings</em>, each unpacking a different aspect of the same geometric fact.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">V.2 Why Both Channels Are Needed</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The two quantum formulations require structurally different content from the foundation. The Hamiltonian formulation requires algebraic-symmetry content: a Hilbert space carrying unitary representations of a translation group, with Stone&#8217;s theorem generating the momentum operator. The Lagrangian formulation requires geometric-propagation content: a wavefront structure on spacetime, with Huygens&#8217; secondary wavelets generating the path-summation representation. A foundation that provides only one of these contents can generate only one of the quantum formulations (as derived result); it can reach the other formulation only through the equivalence theorems established after the fact (Feynman 1948, Stone-von Neumann 1931-1932), but not as an independent derivation from the foundation itself.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">A foundation with <em>both</em> channels can generate both formulations as independent derivations. dx₄/dt = ic is such a foundation. Its algebraic-symmetry content generates the Hamiltonian route; its geometric-propagation content generates the Lagrangian route. The derivations are independent — disjoint intermediate structures, disjoint mathematical machinery — because they use disjoint contents of the principle.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">V.3 The Unique Dual-Channel Property</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The uniqueness claim of this section is as follows: dx₄/dt = ic is the only known candidate foundation for quantum mechanics whose single geometric statement contains both channels simultaneously. This section develops the claim through a systematic analysis of candidate foundations and their channel structure.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Classical Lagrangian variational mechanics.</strong> The principle of least action δS = 0 combined with a classical Lagrangian L(q, q̇, t) generates Newton&#8217;s equations through the Euler-Lagrange equations. This is a variational principle with propagation content (paths extremize the action), but it does not generate a symmetry structure on a Hilbert space — the quantum operator formalism is not derived, and the commutation relations are not consequences of the principle. Classical Lagrangian mechanics has content analogous to Channel B (propagation) but lacks the algebraic-symmetry content of Channel A. To quantize classical Lagrangian mechanics, additional structure (canonical quantization, or geometric quantization, or path-integral quantization) must be imposed. The principle of least action does not by itself generate quantum mechanics.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Classical symplectic geometry.</strong> A phase space (M, ω) with symplectic form ω supports Hamiltonian dynamics through the vector field X_H defined by ω(X_H, ·) = dH, for a Hamiltonian function H: M → ℝ. This is an algebraic structure on phase space that supports the Hamiltonian formulation of classical mechanics (Poisson brackets, canonical transformations, phase flow). Geometric quantization [Kostant 1970; Souriau 1970; Woodhouse 1992] constructs a Hilbert space and quantum operators from (M, ω) through prequantization (a line bundle with curvature ω), polarization (a foliation selecting half the coordinates), and metaplectic correction. This generates the Hamiltonian operator formulation with the factor i in the commutators and the factor ℏ as the scale of the prequantization line bundle&#8217;s curvature. Classical symplectic geometry has content analogous to Channel A (algebraic-symmetry, through the symplectic form&#8217;s algebraic structure) but lacks the geometric-propagation content of Channel B — the path integral does not emerge naturally from symplectic geometry; it must be constructed separately (the Feynman-Kac formula from stochastic analysis, or a path-integral quantization applied after the fact). Geometric quantization reaches the Hamiltonian formulation but does not reach the Lagrangian formulation as an independent derivation; both formulations are equivalent at the end (by Stone-von Neumann and by Feynman&#8217;s proof that the path integral reproduces the Schrödinger equation), but only one is derived from geometric quantization&#8217;s starting point.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Stochastic dynamics (Nelson, Lindgren-Liukkonen).</strong> Stochastic mechanics postulates that particles undergo a classical-mechanical motion with superimposed Brownian-like fluctuations whose diffusion coefficient is ℏ/(2m). From the resulting drift-diffusion equations, the Schrödinger equation is derived. Lindgren-Liukkonen&#8217;s stochastic optimal control requires a Lorentz-invariant stochastic action, which forces the Lagrangian to be imaginary (√(det g) = i in Minkowski signature) and the noise variance to be imaginary (σ² = i/m). This generates the Schrödinger equation through a route analogous to Channel B (propagation, via stochastic fluctuations), but does not generate the operator formalism as an independent derivation. The stochastic frameworks have partial Channel B content but lack Channel A content; they reach the Schrödinger equation, but they do not separately derive the canonical commutation relation or the Hilbert-space representation structure. Moreover, Nelson&#8217;s framework takes ℏ as an input to the diffusion coefficient rather than deriving it, and Lindgren-Liukkonen takes the analytic continuation (the i) as a mathematical requirement of Lorentz invariance rather than identifying it with a physical fact.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Geometric algebra (Hestenes).</strong> Hestenes&#8217;s spacetime algebra reinterprets i as a bivector iσ₃ = γ₂γ₁ in Cl(1,3), giving i a static geometric identity. The Dirac equation, the CCR, and the Schrödinger equation can all be reformulated in Hestenes&#8217;s algebra. But geometric algebra is a reinterpretation of existing physics, not a derivation of quantum mechanics from a deeper principle: the Dirac equation, the CCR, and the Schrödinger equation are inputs to be reformulated rather than outputs to be derived. Hestenes&#8217;s framework has static Channel-B-like content (geometric identity of i) but lacks a dynamical principle that would generate either Channel A or Channel B from deeper structure.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Trace dynamics (Adler).</strong> Adler&#8217;s program derives quantum mechanics as a statistical thermodynamic average of a deeper classical matrix dynamics. The CCR emerges as a Ward identity of the canonical ensemble&#8217;s equipartition theorem: ⟨C̃⟩ = iℏ𝟙. This has partial Channel A content (algebraic structure, inherited from the matrix dynamics) but the i is taken as built into the matrix algebra rather than derived, and ℏ emerges as a temperature-like parameter of the equilibrium rather than as a geometric quantum of action. Trace dynamics does not generate the path integral as an independent derivation; it is one-directional from matrix dynamics to quantum mechanics via statistical averaging.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Cellular automata (&#8216;t Hooft).</strong> &#8216;t Hooft proposes that quantum mechanics emerges from a deterministic cellular automaton at the Planck scale. The Schrödinger equation and CCR are emergent features of the automaton-to-quantum mapping. This has neither Channel A nor Channel B content in the sense developed here: the principle is discrete-determinism rather than continuous-geometric, and the i and ℏ of quantum mechanics are emergent features rather than derivations from the underlying structure.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Bohmian mechanics.</strong> Bohm&#8217;s hidden-variable formulation supplements the Schrödinger equation with a guiding equation for particle trajectories. Both the Schrödinger equation and the guiding equation are postulates; Bohmian mechanics is an interpretation of existing quantum mechanics, not a derivation from a deeper principle.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Loop quantum gravity (Ashtekar and successors).</strong> Ashtekar&#8217;s formulation of general relativity in terms of connection variables provides a Hamiltonian formulation of gravity whose canonical quantization generates the spin-network structure of loop quantum gravity. This is a Hamiltonian-formulation derivation within gravity but does not generate the Lagrangian path integral as an independent derivation; moreover, the starting point (Ashtekar&#8217;s connection variables) is itself a classical Hamiltonian reformulation of general relativity rather than a deeper geometric principle that would generate both formulations independently.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Twistor theory (Penrose and Witten).</strong> Penrose&#8217;s twistor theory and Witten&#8217;s twistor string reformulate spacetime physics in terms of the complex projective space CP³. This is a geometric reformulation with elegant mathematical properties (Proposition X.1 of [MG-Twistor]), but twistor theory does not by itself generate the Hamiltonian and Lagrangian formulations of quantum mechanics from a principle; the quantum-mechanical content is imported from standard physics and reformulated in twistor variables.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Constructive gravity (Schuller).</strong> Schuller&#8217;s 2020 constructive-gravity programme [arXiv:2003.09726] shows that given the matter Lagrangians&#8217; principal polynomials, the compatible gravitational dynamics is uniquely determined as the Einstein-Hilbert action. This is a <em>consequence</em> derivation — given matter, determine gravity uniquely — which presupposes the matter Lagrangians already in Lagrangian form. Schuller&#8217;s programme has partial Channel B content (propagation structure determined by principal polynomials) but does not generate the quantum-mechanical Hamiltonian formulation as an independent derivation; the CCR and operator algebra are inputs from quantum-mechanical matter Lagrangians rather than outputs from the constructive-gravity starting point.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Weinberg&#8217;s Lagrangian QFT.</strong> Weinberg&#8217;s derivation requires the S-matrix to be Lorentz-invariant and to satisfy cluster decomposition, from which he derives the necessity of quantum fields (as cluster-decomposition-compatible Fourier modes of creation/annihilation operators) and the Lagrangian formalism as the natural generator of Lorentz-invariant S-matrix elements. This presupposes the operator formalism (commutation relations for bosons, anticommutation for fermions, unitary representation of the Poincaré group) and derives the Lagrangian on top of it. Weinberg&#8217;s approach is <em>one-directional</em>: from operator formalism to Lagrangian, not from a common foundation to both. Moreover, Lorentz invariance and cluster decomposition are themselves physical postulates.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The pattern across these candidate foundations is that each possesses partial content matching one channel but not the other, or possesses neither channel but instead a different structure (emergent, interpretational, reinterpretational). No prior candidate foundation for quantum mechanics possesses both channels simultaneously in a single geometric-dynamical statement.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">V.4 Why dx₄/dt = ic Is the Unique Dual-Channel Foundation</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The uniqueness of dx₄/dt = ic among candidate quantum foundations — in possessing both channels simultaneously — is not accidental. It reflects the specific structure of the geometric statement: <em>a physical axis advancing at the velocity of light, uniformly from every point, spherically symmetrically.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The uniformity of the advance (rate ic at every point, invariant under spacetime translations and Lorentz boosts) is the algebraic-symmetry content. The spherical symmetry of the advance (isotropic expansion from each point, generating Huygens&#8217; secondary wavelets at each point) is the geometric-propagation content. The two contents are logically independent — one can imagine (pathologically) a principle with uniform-rate non-spherical advance, or spherical advance at non-uniform rate — but they are both required components of dx₄/dt = ic as a physical principle describing the dynamics of a real geometric axis. The reason the principle has both contents is that real physical axes in three-dimensional space, when they advance, must advance <em>from every point</em> (uniformity) <em>in every direction</em> (isotropy), because space is homogeneous and isotropic. The dual-channel content of dx₄/dt = ic is therefore <em>forced by the minimal physical interpretation of the mathematical statement</em>: a geometric axis advancing at rate ic must advance uniformly and spherically, and these are the two channels.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">This is why no prior candidate foundation has the dual-channel property. Classical symplectic geometry is an algebraic structure on phase space; it has no &#8220;advance&#8221; of any kind, so it cannot have the spherical-propagation content. Stochastic dynamics is a time-evolution structure on configuration space; it has propagation content but no symmetry structure built into its starting postulate. Geometric algebra is a static reformulation of Minkowski spacetime; it has algebraic structure but no dynamics. &#8216;t Hooft&#8217;s cellular automaton is a discrete-determinism structure; it has neither continuous propagation nor continuous symmetry content.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">dx₄/dt = ic is the first candidate foundation for quantum mechanics whose statement is both <em>geometric</em> (specifying a real physical axis in spacetime) and <em>dynamical</em> (specifying the advance of that axis at a definite rate), with the minimal physical interpretation of that statement requiring both channels simultaneously. It is the first candidate foundation whose dual-channel content makes the two-route derivation possible.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">V.5 The Compression of Physics</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The dual-channel structure identified here has a deeper implication for the foundational structure of physics. The Hamiltonian and Lagrangian formulations of quantum mechanics are not merely alternative mathematical frameworks for the same physics; they are the two distinct algebraic expressions of the two distinct informational channels of a single underlying geometric fact. When the standard textbook presents them as alternatives, it obscures the fact that they are theorems of one principle. When the McGucken Principle presents both as consequences, it reveals the fact that they are not alternatives — they are shadows of a single deeper structure cast in two different mathematical languages.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">This is the compression of physics that a correct foundation accomplishes. Two formulations collapse to one principle. Every factor of i and every factor of ℏ in both formulations collapses to a single geometric fact: the perpendicularity and the oscillation-scale of x₄&#8217;s advance. The operator formalism and the path-integral formalism collapse to two readings of one equation. Einstein&#8217;s standard — &#8220;the more impressive the greater the simplicity of its premises, the more different kinds of things it relates, and the more extended is its area of applicability&#8221; — is met: one equation dx₄/dt = ic relates the full algebraic content of the operator formulation, the full propagation content of the path-integral formulation, and every derived structure of quantum mechanics from the Schrödinger equation to the canonical commutation relation to the uncertainty principle to the Born rule to the Dirac equation. The simplicity of the premise and the range of applicability stand in the relation Einstein identified as the mark of a correct theory.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">V.6 Wave/Particle Duality as the Dual-Channel Reading at the Ontological Level</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The dual-channel analysis of §V.1 was developed to explain why the McGucken Principle generates both the Hamiltonian and Lagrangian <em>formulations</em> of quantum mechanics. The same analysis applies, with a single structural shift in focus, to explain the phenomenon that has served as the central philosophical puzzle of quantum mechanics for the past century: <em>wave/particle duality</em>.</p>



<h4 class="wp-block-heading">V.6.1 The Century-Old Puzzle</h4>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Wave/particle duality is the empirical observation that quantum objects — photons, electrons, neutrons, atoms, and in modern experiments molecules up to C_60 and larger — simultaneously exhibit wave-like behavior (interference in double-slit geometries, diffraction through apertures, coherent superposition) and particle-like behavior (localized detection events, discrete energy and momentum exchange, quantized absorption and emission). The duality was identified in its modern form by Einstein in his 1905 photoelectric paper [Einstein 1905a], which established that light exhibits particle-like energy quantization E = hν even though Young&#8217;s 1801 double-slit experiment had established its wave-like interference. It was extended to matter by de Broglie&#8217;s 1924 doctoral dissertation [de Broglie 1924], which postulated that every particle of mass m and momentum p has an associated wavelength λ = h/p — the <em>de Broglie relation</em> — and was confirmed experimentally by the Davisson-Germer 1927 electron-diffraction observations [Davisson-Germer 1927] and the Thomson 1928 electron-diffraction images [Thomson 1928].</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The theoretical response to the duality across the ninety-eight years since de Broglie has been, almost uniformly, to treat it as a <em>puzzle to be interpreted</em>. Bohr&#8217;s 1927 Como lecture [Bohr 1928] introduced the <em>principle of complementarity</em>: wave and particle aspects are <em>incompatible</em> descriptions applying in mutually exclusive experimental contexts, and a complete description of any quantum experiment requires specifying which context is realized. Heisenberg&#8217;s 1927 uncertainty relation [Heisenberg 1927] supplied the quantitative complement to complementarity: sharp position (particle aspect) and sharp momentum (wave aspect) cannot be simultaneously specified, with the trade-off Δx·Δp ≥ ℏ/2. Born&#8217;s 1926 probabilistic interpretation [Born 1926a] mediated between the two aspects by treating the wavefunction&#8217;s squared modulus as the probability density for particle localization, so that the wave evolves deterministically until measurement forces a particle-like outcome.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Alternative interpretations arose in response to the perceived inadequacy of Copenhagen&#8217;s &#8220;complementarity&#8221; as an explanation: de Broglie&#8217;s pilot-wave interpretation [de Broglie 1927], developed by Bohm [Bohm 1952] and extended by Dürr-Goldstein-Zanghì [Dürr-Goldstein-Zanghì 1992] and others, treats particles as guided by a physically real wavefunction; Everett&#8217;s relative-state formulation [Everett 1957], developed into the many-worlds interpretation by DeWitt [DeWitt 1970] and Wallace [Wallace 2012], treats the wavefunction as physically real with particle-like localization corresponding to branches of the universal wavefunction; Wheeler&#8217;s delayed-choice experiments [Wheeler 1978, 1984], realized experimentally by Jacques et al. [Jacques 2007], sharpen the puzzle by showing that the decision to treat a photon as &#8220;particle&#8221; or &#8220;wave&#8221; can be made after it has already traversed the apparatus; Scully and Drühl&#8217;s quantum-eraser analysis [Scully-Drühl 1982], realized experimentally by Kim et al. [Kim 2000] and Walborn et al. [Walborn 2002], further sharpens the puzzle by showing that &#8220;which-path&#8221; information can be erased after the fact, restoring interference.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Modern experimental work has established the duality in ever more exotic regimes. Zeilinger&#8217;s program has observed interference for neutrons [Rauch-Werner 1975; Summhammer 1983], atoms [Kasevich-Chu 1991; Keith 1991], C_60 and C_70 fullerenes [Arndt 1999; Nairz-Arndt-Zeilinger 2003], phthalocyanine [Juffmann 2012], and even larger molecules up to ~2000 atomic mass units [Fein 2019]. In every such experiment, the quantum object behaves as a wave (producing an interference pattern) when propagating through the apparatus <em>and</em> as a particle (producing a discrete detection event at a specific pixel of the detector) when observed. The duality is universal, not restricted to &#8220;pure&#8221; photons or electrons; it is a structural feature of quantum mechanics at every mass scale accessible to experiment.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Across the ninety-eight years of theoretical engagement with wave/particle duality, no prior framework has dissolved the puzzle — each has interpreted, reframed, or accepted the duality as an irreducible feature of quantum reality, but none has explained <em>why</em> quantum objects must simultaneously exhibit both aspects, and none has derived the two aspects as simultaneous consequences of a single deeper geometric principle.</p>



<h4 class="wp-block-heading">V.6.2 The Dual-Channel Reading</h4>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Under the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic, wave/particle duality is not a puzzle requiring interpretation; it is the direct consequence of the Principle&#8217;s dual-channel content, applied at the ontological level.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Channel B (geometric-propagation) generates the wave aspect.</strong> The spherical symmetry of x₄&#8217;s expansion from every spacetime point is, by Proposition L.1, Huygens&#8217; principle — every spacetime point is the center of a secondary wavelet, and iterated Huygens composition (Proposition L.2) generates wave-front propagation through spacetime. The interference patterns observed in the double-slit experiment are the constructive and destructive superposition of these Huygens wavelets from the two slits. The diffraction patterns observed in single-slit geometries are the same Huygens wavelets expanded from each point of the slit aperture. The matter-wave wavelength λ_dB = h/p observed in Davisson-Germer, Thomson, and all subsequent matter-wave experiments is, by the derivation of [MG-deBroglie, Theorem 4], the x₄-phase accumulation rate of matter per unit of spatial motion — the Compton-frequency coupling of Channel B producing oscillatory wavefronts with the specific wavelength that de Broglie postulated in 1924. The wave aspect of quantum objects is <em>entirely</em> the Channel B reading of dx₄/dt = ic: propagating wavefronts produced by iterated x₄-sphere expansion from every spacetime point.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Channel A (algebraic-symmetry) generates the particle aspect.</strong> The invariance of x₄&#8217;s advance under spacetime translations is, by Proposition H.2, Stone&#8217;s theorem applied to the translation group — and the self-adjoint generators of those translations are, by Proposition H.3, the four-momentum operators p̂^μ whose eigenvalues are the localized four-momenta of particle states. The discrete detection events observed at specific pixels of the detector screen are eigenvalue events of the position observable q̂ — sharp eigenvalues at localized spacetime points. The quantized energy and momentum exchanges observed in the photoelectric effect (Einstein 1905), Compton scattering (Compton 1923), and every other &#8220;particle-like&#8221; process are the eigenvalue exchanges of Channel A&#8217;s algebraic observables: discrete values of energy and momentum conserved in individual scattering events. The Heisenberg uncertainty relation Δx·Δp ≥ ℏ/2 — the quantitative expression of wave/particle complementarity — is, by [MG-Uncertainty], a theorem about the Fourier-dual structure of the x₄-phase whose algebraic content is Channel A and whose propagation content is Channel B. The particle aspect of quantum objects is <em>entirely</em> the Channel A reading of dx₄/dt = ic: localized eigenvalue structure generated by the algebraic-symmetry content of x₄&#8217;s advance.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">A photon traveling through a double-slit apparatus <em>does</em> both simultaneously. Its Channel B content is the spherical Huygens wavelets emanating from every spacetime point the photon&#8217;s wavefront reaches — including both slits, producing the interference pattern on the screen. Its Channel A content is the localized detection event at a specific screen pixel — the eigenvalue of the position observable at the moment of detection. Both are real; both are simultaneous; both are consequences of the same dx₄/dt = ic. There is no contradiction because the two readings are not competing descriptions of the same thing — they are two simultaneous readings of one geometric principle, corresponding to two distinct informational contents present in the principle&#8217;s statement.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Applied systematically, the dual-channel reading resolves each of the classical puzzles of wave/particle duality.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>The double-slit puzzle.</strong> Why does the interference pattern require both slits to be open? Channel B reading: because the Huygens wavelets from both slits interfere constructively and destructively at each point of the screen, and closing one slit removes one set of wavelets, destroying the interference. Why does the pattern vanish when which-slit information is obtained? Channel A reading: because a which-slit measurement is an eigenvalue event of the slit-position observable, and an eigenvalue event is a Channel A phenomenon that is structurally orthogonal to the Channel B propagation that produces interference. Under the dual-channel reading, obtaining which-slit information forces the system into Channel A eigenvalue-registration mode, suppressing the Channel B interference. This is not a philosophical puzzle to be interpreted via complementarity; it is the structural consequence of having chosen to read one channel rather than the other at the measurement.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>The delayed-choice puzzle</strong> [Wheeler 1978; Jacques 2007]. Why can the decision to observe wave or particle behavior be made <em>after</em> the photon has traversed the apparatus? Because both readings are simultaneously available at every spacetime point along the photon&#8217;s path, not produced retroactively by the measurement. The photon&#8217;s Channel B wavefront is present throughout the apparatus; the Channel A eigenvalue event is produced at the detector. The &#8220;delayed choice&#8221; is a choice of which channel to read at the final detector, not a retroactive alteration of what happened earlier. Wheeler&#8217;s puzzle dissolves because there was never a moment at which the photon &#8220;had to be&#8221; a wave or a particle; both channels were present throughout, and the detection selects which one to register.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>The quantum-eraser puzzle</strong> [Scully-Drühl 1982; Kim 2000]. Why can interference be restored after which-path information has been obtained and then erased? Because Channel A and Channel B are not temporally ordered — the eigenvalue structure and the wavefront structure coexist at every spacetime point. &#8220;Obtaining&#8221; and &#8220;erasing&#8221; which-path information correspond to operational choices about which channel to correlate with the final detection; they do not alter the underlying dual-channel content of dx₄/dt = ic. The quantum eraser experiments are demonstrations of the simultaneity of the two channels, not of retroactive causation.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>The matter-wave puzzle.</strong> Why do electrons, neutrons, atoms, and molecules exhibit wave behavior with wavelength λ = h/p? Because the Channel B content of dx₄/dt = ic applies to any matter coupled to x₄&#8217;s oscillation, and the de Broglie relation λ = h/p is (by [MG-deBroglie]) the x₄-phase accumulation rate of matter at its Compton frequency ω_C = mc²/ℏ. The matter-wave wavelength is not a mysterious feature specific to quantum mechanics; it is the wavelength of the oscillatory wavefront that every massive particle carries through Channel B of the Principle.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>The molecular-interference puzzle</strong> [Arndt 1999; Fein 2019]. Why do C_60 and larger molecules still exhibit interference despite their large mass and internal structure? Because Channel B applies to every object with non-zero rest mass, with the de Broglie wavelength λ = h/(Mv) scaled by the total mass; for sufficiently low velocities, the wavelength remains comparable to the slit spacing even for very large molecules, and Channel B&#8217;s interference is observable. There is no lower limit to wave behavior imposed by &#8220;quantumness&#8221; because there is no separate &#8220;quantum&#8221; realm — all matter carries Channel B content by virtue of coupling to x₄&#8217;s oscillation.</p>



<h4 class="wp-block-heading">V.6.3 Novelty and Uniqueness at the Ontological Level</h4>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The explanation of wave/particle duality as the dual-channel reading of dx₄/dt = ic is novel in the precise sense that no prior framework has explained both aspects as simultaneous consequences of a single deeper geometric principle. The landscape of prior explanations may be summarized as follows.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Copenhagen (Bohr 1928; Heisenberg 1927).</strong> Complementarity is an interpretational doctrine, not a derivation: it asserts that wave and particle aspects are incompatible and must be specified by experimental context, but it does not derive either aspect from a deeper principle. The uncertainty relation Δx·Δp ≥ ℏ/2 is a theorem of the CCR [q̂, p̂] = iℏ, which Copenhagen takes as a postulate of quantum mechanics.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Pilot-wave (de Broglie 1927; Bohm 1952; Dürr-Goldstein-Zanghì 1992).</strong> The wave aspect is physically real (the wavefunction guides the particle); the particle aspect is physically real (the particle has a definite trajectory). Both are postulates of the framework; the wavefunction&#8217;s Schrödinger dynamics and the guiding equation are both added as independent postulates. The pilot-wave framework does not explain why the wavefunction should exist or why it should have the specific Schrödinger dynamics; both are inherited from standard quantum mechanics.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Many-worlds (Everett 1957; Wallace 2012).</strong> The wavefunction is the only physically real structure; the particle aspect is an emergent feature of branching (decoherence producing effectively-classical branch histories within the universal wavefunction). This explains the particle aspect as a branching phenomenon but takes the wavefunction and its Schrödinger dynamics as postulates. The dual aspect is not derived from a deeper principle.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Decoherence (Zurek 1982; Joos-Zeh 1985).</strong> Environmental decoherence explains the <em>appearance</em> of classical particle-like behavior in macroscopic systems by tracing out environment degrees of freedom, producing an effective classical distribution from quantum superposition. This is structurally important for understanding the classical limit but does not explain why the underlying quantum structure exhibits dual aspect at the microscopic level; the wavefunction and its dynamics are again postulates.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Relational quantum mechanics (Rovelli 1996).</strong> Quantum states are relational — they describe correlations between systems rather than intrinsic properties — and the wave/particle distinction is a relational artifact. This is a philosophical reframing, not a derivation from a deeper physical principle.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>QBism (Fuchs-Mermin-Schack 2014).</strong> The wavefunction is an epistemic object describing an agent&#8217;s personal probability assignments. Wave/particle duality is a feature of the probabilistic structure, not an ontological feature of physical systems. This is an epistemological reinterpretation, not a derivation from a deeper physical principle.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Stochastic mechanics (Nelson 1966; Lindgren-Liukkonen 2019).</strong> The wave aspect is derived from stochastic dynamics in configuration space; the particle aspect is the underlying classical particle position undergoing stochastic fluctuations. The i of the Schrödinger equation is, in these frameworks, an analytic continuation of the real-valued stochastic process, with no physical interpretation of why the continuation should be performed (as noted in §VI.3, §VI.4). The frameworks derive the wave aspect through a single route but do not explain the dual aspect as two readings of a single geometric fact.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Bohmian field-theoretic extensions and others.</strong> A number of frameworks (Bohm-Hiley 1993 implicate-order analysis; Valentini 2004 quantum nonequilibrium program; Struyve 2011 and Dürr 2014 on Bohmian quantum field theory) extend pilot-wave mechanics to fields and gauge theories. These extensions preserve the pilot-wave framework&#8217;s status as an interpretation rather than a derivation; they do not alter the fundamental status of the wave and the particle as postulated rather than derived.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Across the ninety-eight years of theoretical engagement with wave/particle duality, <em>none</em> of these frameworks explains wave and particle as simultaneous readings of a single geometric-dynamical principle, with the wave aspect forced by one channel of that principle&#8217;s content and the particle aspect forced by the other. The McGucken Principle is the first framework to achieve this dual-channel reading. This uniqueness is structural: no framework in the historical record possesses dual-channel content in a single geometric-dynamical statement (as established in §V.3), and therefore no framework can generate both aspects as simultaneous consequences of a single principle. The McGucken framework is the first to do so because it is the first to possess the requisite dual-channel structure.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The explanation is novel in a further sense: it <em>dissolves</em> the puzzle rather than merely interpreting it. Complementarity, pilot waves, many worlds, decoherence, relational states, epistemic reinterpretation — each prior approach accepts the dual aspect as an irreducible feature and proposes an interpretive scheme for living with it. The McGucken reading does not accept the duality as irreducible; it derives the duality as the consequence of a single geometric fact. The puzzle of wave/particle duality, under the McGucken Principle, is not a puzzle at all — it is the expected consequence of having a principle with dual-channel content, applied at the ontological level to the question of what quantum objects <em>are</em>.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading">V.7 Schrödinger and Heisenberg Pictures as Dual-Channel Readings at the Dynamical Level</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The dual-channel structure of dx₄/dt = ic applies a third time, at the <em>dynamical</em> level, to the distinction between the Schrödinger and Heisenberg pictures of quantum-mechanical time evolution. Where §§II–III develop the dual reading at the <em>foundational</em> level (Hamiltonian versus Lagrangian formulation) and §V.6 develops it at the <em>ontological</em> level (wave versus particle aspect), this section develops the reading at the dynamical level.</p>



<h4 class="wp-block-heading">V.7.1 The Schrödinger and Heisenberg Pictures</h4>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Quantum-mechanical time evolution admits two equivalent representations. In the <em>Schrödinger picture</em>, physical states |ψ(t)⟩ evolve in time under the Schrödinger equation</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>iℏ d|ψ(t)⟩/dt = Ĥ |ψ(t)⟩,</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">while operators Â are fixed. Expectation values are ⟨ψ(t)| Â |ψ(t)⟩, with all time dependence carried by the state. In the <em>Heisenberg picture</em>, operators Â(t) evolve in time under the Heisenberg equation</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>iℏ dÂ(t)/dt = [Â(t), Ĥ],</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">while states |ψ⟩ are fixed. Expectation values are ⟨ψ| Â(t) |ψ⟩, with all time dependence carried by the operator. The two pictures are related by the unitary transformation Û(t) = exp(−i Ĥ t/ℏ): states in the Schrödinger picture are Û(t) times states in the Heisenberg picture, and operators in the Heisenberg picture are Û(t)† Â Û(t) of operators in the Schrödinger picture. They agree on all expectation values and therefore on all measurable quantities.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The historical development of the two pictures is as follows. Heisenberg&#8217;s 1925 matrix-mechanics paper [Heisenberg 1925] formulated quantum mechanics as an algebra of operators whose matrix elements describe transitions between stationary states; the picture is fundamentally algebraic, with observables as the primary dynamical objects. The algebra was worked out in full by Born, Heisenberg, and Jordan in the <em>Dreimännerarbeit</em> [Born-Heisenberg-Jordan 1926], which established the matrix-mechanical framework with operators satisfying [q̂, p̂] = iℏ and time evolution generated by commutators with the Hamiltonian. Schrödinger&#8217;s 1926 four-paper series [Schrödinger 1926a,b,c,d] formulated quantum mechanics as a wave theory, with physical states represented by wavefunctions ψ(x, t) evolving according to the Schrödinger equation; the picture is fundamentally propagational, with wavefunctions as the primary dynamical objects. The equivalence of the two formulations was established by Schrödinger himself in his third 1926 paper [Schrödinger 1926b], which showed that the matrix elements of Heisenberg&#8217;s operators equal the integrals ∫ψ<em>(x)·Â·ψ(x)dx of Schrödinger&#8217;s wavefunctions, and by von Neumann&#8217;s 1932 </em>Mathematische Grundlagen der Quantenmechanik* [von Neumann 1932], which placed both formulations on the common algebraic foundation of operators on Hilbert space.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Since 1932, the two pictures have coexisted in the physics literature as equivalent representational tools, each adopted in contexts where it is most convenient. The Schrödinger picture is standard for introductory treatments of quantum mechanics (e.g., Sakurai [Sakurai 1994], Griffiths [Griffiths 2005], Cohen-Tannoudji-Diu-Laloë [Cohen-Tannoudji 1977], Messiah [Messiah 1961]) because it makes explicit the role of the wavefunction and the computational simplicity of diagonalizing the Hamiltonian; the Heisenberg picture is standard for quantum field theory (e.g., Weinberg [Weinberg 1995], Peskin-Schroeder [Peskin-Schroeder 1995]) because it makes explicit the role of the operator algebra and the covariance of operators under Lorentz transformations. The interaction picture, developed in the 1940s by Tomonaga [Tomonaga 1946], Schwinger [Schwinger 1948], and Feynman [Feynman 1949] for renormalization in quantum electrodynamics, is a hybrid picture in which part of the Hamiltonian acts on states (as in Schrödinger) and part acts on operators (as in Heisenberg); it is the standard workhorse for perturbative QFT calculations but does not add a new structural dimension beyond the Schrödinger/Heisenberg dichotomy.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The question of <em>why</em> these two pictures exist as equivalent representations — why quantum mechanics should admit two structurally distinct formulations of dynamics — has not been addressed in the literature. Textbook treatments present the equivalence as a mathematical fact (Schrödinger 1926b proved it; Stone-von Neumann confirmed that the representation is essentially unique) without explaining why the dynamical content of quantum mechanics should decompose into state-dynamics and operator-dynamics as two readings of a single underlying dynamics. The McGucken Principle supplies that explanation.</p>



<h4 class="wp-block-heading">V.7.2 The Dual-Channel Reading at the Dynamical Level</h4>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Under the dual-channel analysis of §§V.1–V.5, the Schrödinger and Heisenberg pictures are not arbitrary representational choices — they are the two readings of the dynamical content of dx₄/dt = ic.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Heisenberg picture as Channel A at the dynamical level.</strong> Time evolution in the Heisenberg picture is generated by commutators with the Hamiltonian: dÂ/dt = (i/ℏ)[Ĥ, Â]. This is the algebraic-symmetry reading of time evolution. The Hamiltonian Ĥ is the self-adjoint generator of time-translation, by Stone&#8217;s theorem applied to the time-translation subgroup of the Poincaré group (exactly parallel to the application in Proposition H.2 for spatial translations). Just as Stone&#8217;s theorem applied to spatial translations forces the momentum operator p̂ = −iℏ ∂/∂q as the generator of spatial translations (Proposition H.3), Stone&#8217;s theorem applied to time translations forces the Hamiltonian Ĥ = iℏ ∂/∂t as the generator of time translations. The Heisenberg equation iℏ dÂ/dt = [Â, Ĥ] is the direct consequence of this structure: operators evolve under the action of the time-translation generator, with the factor iℏ on the left-hand side and the factor iℏ in the Stone&#8217;s-theorem construction of Ĥ both inherited from dx₄/dt = ic by the same derivational chain that yields the CCR in Proposition H.4. The Heisenberg picture is the Channel A reading of dynamics: algebraic-symmetry structure with observables as the primary objects and time evolution as a commutator flow on the operator algebra.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Schrödinger picture as Channel B at the dynamical level.</strong> Time evolution in the Schrödinger picture is generated by the Schrödinger equation iℏ ∂ψ/∂t = Ĥ ψ. This is the geometric-propagation reading of time evolution. By Proposition L.5, the Schrödinger equation is the non-relativistic limit of the path-integral propagation of the wavefunction through spacetime, with the wavefunction&#8217;s time evolution being the continuum limit of iterated Huygens-spherical-expansion composition. The wavefunction ψ(x, t) is, literally, the three-dimensional cross-section of x₄&#8217;s expanding wavefront at observer time t — the McGucken Sphere of [MG-Master, Part IV] projected onto the spatial dimensions. The Schrödinger equation describes how this wavefront advances: the wavefunction at time t + dt is the Huygens-propagated wavefunction at time t, with the specific form of the propagator determined by the Compton-frequency coupling of matter to x₄&#8217;s oscillation (Proposition L.3). The Schrödinger picture is the Channel B reading of dynamics: geometric-propagation structure with wavefunctions as the primary objects and time evolution as a wavefront propagation through spacetime.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The two pictures are therefore not arbitrary equivalent formulations — they are the two readings of the Principle&#8217;s dual-channel content applied to the dynamical question &#8220;how does quantum mechanics evolve in time?&#8221; Channel A supplies the algebraic-symmetry reading (Heisenberg: commutator flow on the operator algebra). Channel B supplies the geometric-propagation reading (Schrödinger: wavefront propagation of the state). The equivalence of the two pictures, established in 1926 by Schrödinger and 1932 by von Neumann and secured representation-theoretically by Stone-von Neumann, is the mathematical expression of the two readings of the same dynamical content.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The correspondence between the four levels of dual-channel structure — with the fourth level (locality/nonlocality) developed in §V.8 — may be summarized as follows:</p>



<figure class="wp-block-table"><table class="has-fixed-layout"><thead><tr><th>Level of description</th><th>Channel A (algebraic-symmetry)</th><th>Channel B (geometric-propagation)</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Foundational (§§II–III)</td><td>Hamiltonian operator formulation</td><td>Lagrangian path-integral formulation</td></tr><tr><td>Dynamical (§V.7)</td><td>Heisenberg picture (operators evolve)</td><td>Schrödinger picture (states evolve)</td></tr><tr><td>Ontological (§V.6)</td><td>Particle aspect (localized eigenvalues)</td><td>Wave aspect (propagating wavefronts)</td></tr></tbody></table></figure>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The four levels are not independent; they track the same dual-channel structure of dx₄/dt = ic applied to four distinct physics questions (what is the correct formulation of quantum mechanics? how does quantum mechanics evolve in time? what are quantum objects? how do quantum objects relate to spacetime causal structure?). The McGucken Principle answers all four with the same dual-channel structure: two simultaneous readings of one geometric fact.</p>



<h4 class="wp-block-heading">V.7.3 A Geometric Proof of the Schrödinger-Heisenberg Equivalence from dx₄/dt = ic</h4>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The dual-channel reading of §V.7.2 supplies more than an identification of which picture corresponds to which channel. It supplies the basis for a <em>geometric proof</em> of the equivalence of the two pictures, distinct from the algebraic proofs of Schrödinger [Schrödinger 1926b] and von Neumann [von Neumann 1932], in which the equivalence follows from the physical content of x₄&#8217;s advance rather than from mathematical manipulation of the unitary evolution operator. This subsection develops the proof in formal theorem-lemma-corollary structure and explains the sense in which it is geometric rather than algebraic.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Theorem V.7.3</strong> <em>(Schrödinger-Heisenberg equivalence from dx₄/dt = ic).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>Let ℋ be the complex Hilbert space of quantum states of a physical system, let Ĥ be the system&#8217;s Hamiltonian operator (the self-adjoint generator of time translations on ℋ), let Û(t) = exp(−iĤt/ℏ) be the one-parameter unitary evolution group on ℋ, let |ψ⟩ ∈ ℋ be any state, and let Â be any self-adjoint observable on ℋ. Define the Schrödinger-picture state at time t as |ψ_S(t)⟩ = Û(t)|ψ⟩ with the observable Â held fixed, and the Heisenberg-picture observable at time t as Â_H(t) = Û(t)† Â Û(t) with the state |ψ⟩ held fixed. Under the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic, the equality of expectation values</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>⟨ψ_S(t)| Â |ψ_S(t)⟩ = ⟨ψ| Â_H(t) |ψ⟩</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>holds not as an algebraic identity of unitary conjugation but as a direct consequence of the geometric content of dx₄/dt = ic: both sides compute the expectation of the same observable after the same physical process (x₄&#8217;s advance by amount ic·t from every spacetime point) has occurred, with the two sides differing only in which mathematical object (the state vector on the left, the observable on the right) is updated to reflect the advance while the other is held fixed.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The proof proceeds through three lemmas establishing, respectively, the physical interpretation of the unitary Û(t) as the Hilbert-space implementation of x₄&#8217;s advance (Lemma V.7.3.A), the equivalence of Channel B and Channel A readings of the advance at the level of individual Hilbert-space objects (Lemma V.7.3.B), and the preservation of expectation values under either reading (Lemma V.7.3.C). The theorem then follows from Lemma V.7.3.C applied to the specific state and observable of the theorem statement.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Lemma V.7.3.A</strong> <em>(The unitary evolution operator as Hilbert-space implementation of x₄&#8217;s advance).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>The one-parameter unitary group Û(t) = exp(−iĤt/ℏ) on the Hilbert space ℋ is the operator implementation of x₄&#8217;s advance by amount ic·t. Specifically: (i) the self-adjoint generator Ĥ is the Noether charge associated with x₄&#8217;s temporal uniformity [MG-Noether, Propositions IV.1–IV.2]; (ii) the factor i in the exponent is the perpendicularity marker of x₄ (same i as in x₄ = ict, propagated through Stone&#8217;s theorem on the time-translation subgroup as in Proposition H.2 applied to spatial translations); (iii) the factor ℏ is the action per complete x₄-oscillation cycle at the Planck frequency [MG-Constants, §V]. Both constants in Û(t) are therefore inherited from dx₄/dt = ic by the derivational chain established in Propositions H.1–H.3, with time translations playing the role played by spatial translations in those propositions.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proof of Lemma V.7.3.A.</strong> On the complex Hilbert space ℋ, the one-parameter unitary group representing time translations is strongly continuous (physical predictions depend continuously on time), so by Stone&#8217;s theorem [Stone 1932], Û(t) = exp(−itÂ/ℏ) for some densely-defined self-adjoint generator Â. Writing Â = Ĥ and calling Ĥ the Hamiltonian, we have Û(t) = exp(−iĤt/ℏ). The factor i is the Stone&#8217;s-theorem factor, forced by the combined requirements of unitarity (Û(t)Û(t)† = 𝟙) and self-adjointness of the generator (Ĥ = Ĥ†). Geometrically, the factor i in the exponent is the same i as in the integrated form x₄ = ict of the McGucken Principle: in both cases, i marks the perpendicularity of x₄ to the three spatial dimensions, and the physical dynamical parameter t enters the exponent as ic·t (with the factor c absorbed into Ĥ through dimensional analysis: Ĥ has dimensions of energy = mass × velocity², and ℏ/c has dimensions of action/velocity = mass × length, so Ĥt/ℏ is dimensionless as required). The factor ℏ is identified in [MG-Constants, §V] as the quantum of action per complete x₄-oscillation cycle at the Planck frequency f_P = 1/t_P. The Hamiltonian Ĥ, as Noether charge of the time-translation subgroup of the Poincaré group, is derived in [MG-Noether, Propositions IV.1–IV.2] from the temporal uniformity of x₄&#8217;s advance (dx₄/dt = ic is the same at every spacetime point, so time-translation is a symmetry of the Principle). Both the operator Ĥ and the constants i, ℏ entering the exponent are therefore forced by dx₄/dt = ic; the unitary Û(t) is, as claimed, the Hilbert-space implementation of x₄&#8217;s advance by amount ic·t. ∎</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Lemma V.7.3.B</strong> <em>(The dual-channel action of Û(t) on states and on observables).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>The unitary Û(t) of Lemma V.7.3.A admits two equivalent actions on a quantum mechanical description of the system at time t = 0 to yield the description at time t:</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>(B1) Channel B (geometric-propagation) action: Û(t) acts on the state vector, carrying |ψ⟩ to |ψ_S(t)⟩ = Û(t)|ψ⟩, with observables Â held fixed. Geometrically, this action implements x₄&#8217;s advance as the propagation of the state&#8217;s wavefront forward by amount ic·t in the Feynman path-integral sense of Propositions L.1–L.5.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>(B2) Channel A (algebraic-symmetry) action: Û(t) acts on the observable by conjugation, carrying Â to Â_H(t) = Û(t)†·Â·Û(t), with the state |ψ⟩ held fixed. Geometrically, this action implements x₄&#8217;s advance as the transformation of the observable&#8217;s algebraic definition into the time-translated reference frame, consistent with Heisenberg&#8217;s original [Heisenberg 1925] matrix-mechanical treatment of observables as the primary dynamical objects.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proof of Lemma V.7.3.B.</strong> Both actions are consequences of the fact that Û(t) is unitary, Û(t)Û(t)† = 𝟙. Given any matrix element ⟨φ|Ô|ψ⟩ for an operator Ô and states |ψ⟩, |φ⟩, the insertion of the identity Û(t)Û(t)† between any two factors preserves the matrix element; moving this insertion across the factors redistributes the time evolution across different mathematical objects:</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>⟨φ|Ô|ψ⟩ = ⟨φ|[Û(t)Û(t)†] Ô [Û(t)Û(t)†]|ψ⟩ = [⟨φ|Û(t)][Û(t)† Ô Û(t)][Û(t)†|ψ⟩]</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>= ⟨φ_S(t)|Ô_H(t)|ψ_S(t)⟩,</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">where |ψ_S(t)⟩ = Û(t)|ψ⟩ and |φ_S(t)⟩ = Û(t)|φ⟩ are Schrödinger-picture states (Channel B action on states) and Ô_H(t) = Û(t)†·Ô·Û(t) is the Heisenberg-picture observable (Channel A action on observables). The factorization can be further rearranged by moving Û(t)&#8217;s to adjacent positions:</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>⟨φ|Ô|ψ⟩ = ⟨φ|Ô Û(t)Û(t)†|ψ⟩ = ⟨φ|[Ô] Û(t)|ψ⟩·Û(t)†|ψ⟩⁻¹ · Û(t)†|ψ⟩ = ⟨φ| Ô |ψ_S(t)⟩ → mixed form,</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">or alternatively concentrating Û(t) on one side to obtain the pure Schrödinger or pure Heisenberg reading. The physical content of Lemma V.7.3.B is that x₄&#8217;s advance by amount ic·t — a single physical process — can be mathematically represented by updating either the state (Channel B) or the observable (Channel A) or distributing the update across both (intermediate pictures, including the interaction picture discussed in §V.7.4). The two pure readings (Schrödinger, Heisenberg) are the two extremes of the full dual-channel content; they are equivalent because both implement the same physical x₄-advance on the same system. ∎</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Lemma V.7.3.C</strong> <em>(Preservation of expectation values under both readings).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>For any state |ψ⟩ ∈ ℋ and any observable Â = Â† on ℋ, the expectation value of Â at time t is</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>⟨Â⟩(t) := ⟨ψ_S(t)| Â |ψ_S(t)⟩ = ⟨ψ| Â_H(t) |ψ⟩,</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>independent of whether the Schrödinger or Heisenberg reading is chosen. Both readings yield the same number because both compute the expectation of the same observable on the same physical system after the same physical process (x₄&#8217;s advance by amount ic·t) has occurred.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proof of Lemma V.7.3.C.</strong> Using the unitarity Û(t)Û(t)† = Û(t)†Û(t) = 𝟙 and the definitions |ψ_S(t)⟩ = Û(t)|ψ⟩ and Â_H(t) = Û(t)†·Â·Û(t):</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>⟨ψ_S(t)| Â |ψ_S(t)⟩ = [Û(t)|ψ⟩]† · Â · [Û(t)|ψ⟩]</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>= ⟨ψ| Û(t)† · Â · Û(t) |ψ⟩</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>= ⟨ψ| [Û(t)† Â Û(t)] |ψ⟩</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>= ⟨ψ| Â_H(t) |ψ⟩.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The algebra is identical to the textbook equivalence proof. The <em>geometric content</em> — which the textbook proof does not articulate — is that both sides compute the expectation of Â in the quantum-mechanical description of the system at time t, where &#8220;at time t&#8221; means &#8220;after x₄ has advanced by amount ic·t from every spacetime point of the initial description.&#8221; The left side carries this advance as an update to the state (Channel B reading); the right side carries it as an update to the observable&#8217;s algebraic definition (Channel A reading); the equality of the two sides follows from the fact that both are describing the same physical x₄-advance. ∎</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proof of Theorem V.7.3.</strong> Apply Lemma V.7.3.C to the specific state |ψ⟩ and observable Â of the theorem statement. The equality ⟨ψ_S(t)| Â |ψ_S(t)⟩ = ⟨ψ| Â_H(t) |ψ⟩ is the direct consequence. ∎</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Corollary V.7.3.D</strong> <em>(Picture independence of all measurable predictions).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>All quantum-mechanical predictions derived from expectation values — transition probabilities, scattering amplitudes, correlation functions, time-ordered products, and the entries of the S-matrix — are picture-independent: they can be computed in the Schrödinger picture or the Heisenberg picture or any intermediate hybrid picture (including the Tomonaga-Schwinger-Feynman interaction picture of §V.7.4) and will yield the same numerical values. The independence follows from the dual-channel reading of Lemma V.7.3.B applied iteratively to products of operators and sums of matrix elements, each individual expectation or matrix element preserving by Lemma V.7.3.C.</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Proof of Corollary V.7.3.D.</strong> Every quantum-mechanical prediction can be expressed as a sum, product, or integral of matrix elements of the form ⟨φ| Ô_1(t_1) Ô_2(t_2) ⋯ Ô_n(t_n) |ψ⟩ (time-ordered products), which can in turn be reduced to expectation-value form by polarization identities. Each individual matrix element is picture-independent by Lemmas V.7.3.B–V.7.3.C (insertions of Û(t_k)Û(t_k)† can be distributed across state vectors and operators in any consistent way), and the composed prediction is therefore picture-independent as well. ∎</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Remark V.7.3.1</strong> <em>(The geometric character of the proof).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The proof of Theorem V.7.3 above is <em>algebraically</em> identical to the textbook proofs of the Schrödinger-Heisenberg equivalence found in [Schrödinger 1926b], [von Neumann 1932], [Dirac 1930], [Sakurai 1994, §2.2], and every modern quantum-mechanics textbook: the algebraic step ⟨Û(t)ψ|Â|Û(t)ψ⟩ = ⟨ψ|Û(t)†·Â·Û(t)|ψ⟩ follows from unitarity and is the central manipulation in all such proofs. The <em>geometric</em> character of the present proof lies not in the algebra — the algebra is the same — but in the <em>physical interpretation</em> of the algebraic objects, supplied by Lemmas V.7.3.A and V.7.3.B.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">In the algebraic proofs, the unitary Û(t) is the abstract time-evolution operator whose existence is established by Stone&#8217;s theorem and whose form Û(t) = exp(−iĤt/ℏ) is established by identifying Ĥ as the Hamiltonian. The factor i in the exponent is unmotivated beyond the mathematical requirement that Û(t) be unitary; the factor ℏ is an empirical constant; the Hamiltonian Ĥ is whatever observable plays the role of the generator of time evolution. The equivalence of the Schrödinger and Heisenberg pictures is a consequence of unitarity, a purely algebraic property.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">In the McGucken proof, the unitary Û(t) is identified in Lemma V.7.3.A as the Hilbert-space implementation of x₄&#8217;s advance by amount ic·t. The factor i is the perpendicularity marker of x₄ (the same i as in x₄ = ict, propagated through Stone&#8217;s theorem on time translations). The factor ℏ is the action per x₄-oscillation cycle at the Planck frequency. The Hamiltonian Ĥ is the Noether charge of x₄&#8217;s temporal uniformity. Every element of Û(t) has geometric meaning drawn from the underlying Principle. The dual-channel reading of Lemma V.7.3.B then identifies the Schrödinger action (Û(t) on state) as the Channel B reading of x₄&#8217;s advance and the Heisenberg action (Û(t) conjugating observable) as the Channel A reading. The equivalence of the two pictures is not merely an algebraic consequence of unitarity; it is a <em>geometric</em> consequence of the fact that both readings are describing the same physical process (x₄&#8217;s advance), differing only in which mathematical object (state or observable) is chosen to carry the update.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The distinction between the algebraic and geometric characters of the proof is the distinction between <em>showing</em> and <em>explaining</em> the equivalence. The algebraic proof shows that the equivalence holds: the equations work out. The geometric proof explains why the equivalence holds: because both sides describe the same x₄-advance, through two channels of the Principle&#8217;s dual content.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Remark V.7.3.2</strong> <em>(Historical comparison and novelty).</em></p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The algebraic proof of the Schrödinger-Heisenberg equivalence was first established by Schrödinger in his third 1926 paper [Schrödinger 1926b], which demonstrated that the matrix elements of Heisenberg&#8217;s operators are identical to the ⟨ψ|Â|ψ⟩ integrals of Schrödinger&#8217;s wavefunctions for all observable quantities. Dirac&#8217;s 1927 transformation theory [Dirac 1927] formalized the picture-independence in terms of unitary transformations between representations. Von Neumann&#8217;s 1932 treatise [von Neumann 1932] placed both formulations on the rigorous Hilbert-space foundation, with the Stone-von Neumann theorem [Stone 1932; von Neumann 1931] securing the representation-theoretic uniqueness. These are the three canonical sources for the equivalence, and every subsequent textbook treatment (Sakurai [Sakurai 1994], Griffiths [Griffiths 2005], Cohen-Tannoudji [Cohen-Tannoudji 1977], Messiah [Messiah 1961], Weinberg [Weinberg 1995], Peskin-Schroeder [Peskin-Schroeder 1995]) either reproduces or builds on these proofs without adding structural content.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">None of these proofs — from Schrödinger 1926b through modern textbooks — identifies the unitary Û(t) with a physical object beyond &#8220;the time-evolution operator&#8221;; none identifies the factor i beyond &#8220;the factor required for unitarity&#8221;; none identifies the factor ℏ beyond &#8220;the empirical constant of quantum mechanics&#8221;; none explains why the two pictures should exist as equivalent representations rather than as arbitrary reformulations or as structurally distinct physical theories. The algebraic manipulations establish the equivalence; they do not explain it.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The geometric proof developed in Theorem V.7.3 and its lemmas is the first demonstration of the Schrödinger-Heisenberg equivalence in the ninety-nine-year history since Schrödinger 1926b that identifies the physical content of the unitary evolution operator (x₄&#8217;s advance), the physical origin of i and ℏ in the operator&#8217;s form (perpendicularity of x₄ and action per x₄-cycle), and the geometric reason for the equivalence of the two pictures (dual-channel reading of x₄&#8217;s advance at the dynamical level). The novelty is structural: the algebra is the same, but the interpretation of the algebra is new, and the interpretation <em>explains</em> what the algebra alone can only <em>show</em>.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The geometric proof is, in a precise sense, what Einstein meant when he described a deeper understanding of a theorem as reaching &#8220;the understanding that leaves no question behind&#8221; rather than merely &#8220;the verification that the equation holds&#8221; [paraphrased from Einstein&#8217;s correspondence with Max Born on the epistemological standards of physical theories]. The Schrödinger 1926b proof verifies the equation. The McGucken proof of Theorem V.7.3 leaves no question behind, because both sides of the equation are identified with two readings of a single geometric fact, and the equation&#8217;s validity is identified with the fact that both readings describe the same physical process.</p>



<h4 class="wp-block-heading">V.7.4 The Interaction Picture and Hybrid Readings</h4>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The interaction picture of Tomonaga-Schwinger-Feynman [Tomonaga 1946; Schwinger 1948; Feynman 1949] is a hybrid picture in which part of the Hamiltonian (the free part Ĥ_0) generates time evolution of operators while the remaining part (the interaction part Ĥ_I) generates time evolution of states. Under the dual-channel reading, the interaction picture is not a third independent reading of the dynamics; it is a <em>convenient factorization</em> of the dynamics into a Channel A component (the free-field time evolution of operators under Ĥ_0) and a Channel B component (the interaction-driven time evolution of states under Ĥ_I). The interaction picture is the computational tool that allows the two channels to be treated separately in perturbation theory — a reflection of the dual-channel structure, not an addition to it.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The Dirac-picture analysis in canonical quantum field theory (Dirac 1930, Weinberg 1995) extends the interaction-picture structure to field-theoretic systems, with similar dual-channel interpretation: the free-field propagators are Channel B content (wavefront propagation of the free fields through spacetime), and the interaction vertices are Channel A content (algebraic rules for how operator products couple at interaction points). Again, the dual-channel structure underlies the mathematical structure; the QFT formalism makes both channels computationally explicit.</p>



<h4 class="wp-block-heading">V.7.5 Novelty and Uniqueness at the Dynamical Level</h4>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The explanation of the Schrödinger and Heisenberg pictures as dual-channel readings of dx₄/dt = ic is novel in the same precise sense as §V.6&#8217;s explanation of wave/particle duality: no prior framework has explained the two pictures as simultaneous consequences of a single deeper principle. Prior treatments of the two pictures fall into four categories.</p>
